- **Reach — `pelletRange`, 150 units (37.5 m; b1105et, 2026-11-10 — 100 from 2026-10-25, PT first).** A volley dies once its centre has flown `pelletRange` from the muzzle (despawn reason `range`); before, only the common 2.2 s TTL ended it, 770 units at 350 u/s. And a bot holding a gun that carries the field does not pull the trigger on a target beyond it: the shotguns have no bloom gate (`botMayFire` passes every multi-pellet gun), so until now a shotgun bot with a clear line fired at any distance, and on Compound four of them kept a sky of pellets alive. One field on the three shotgun entries (Units 2, 11, 18), in both unit tables (`shared/src/sim/constants.js` and the client's `UNIT_DATA`); a gun without the field flies and fires as before. The frozen-brain references were re-frozen at b1025a for the hold.
- **The offline mirror on the sim's broadphase (b1025b, user 2026-10-25: "the online SG pellet mechanics, PT offline first").** The shared sim has tested each pellet's step only against the boxes and floors near it since the online shotgun fix; the offline client's volley branch still walked every box and every floor of the map, eight pellets a volley, every frame. On Compound that is 22,666 boxes and 16,878 floors: 1.16 ms per pellet-step measured, about 10 ms a frame with four shotgun bots — the offline "Compound lags with shotguns" report, and why four Aris did not lag (one bolt each). The branch now takes its candidates from `obstaclesNearSegment`, for the walls and for the floors alike; the index returns a superset, and 60,000 random pellet steps on Compound, Town and Dust stopped at the same walls and floors both ways. The single-round branch still scans the whole list (one segment per round, so a third of the cost at most); online play never had the problem, the server runs the shared sim.

### Aris (Unit 7) — flight & laser bolts

- **Flight kit**: a jump tap in the air re-fires the jump impulse (12 boost per pop, no cooldown); *holding* jump sustains a climb at sprint speed; air-sprint flies **level** (dedicated fly art); the air-dodge holds altitude. Boost does not regen while airborne — altitude is a spent resource.
- Sprinting into a jump **carries the sprint momentum** through the air.
- Her shot is a **64-unit-long laser bolt**: the thin cyan cylinder you see *is* the hitbox (both derive from one spec entry). It grows out of the muzzle — the body never reaches behind the spawn point — and hits with its whole length, so a dodge must clear the entire passing beam, not just its nose.

### Sniper charge & sprint-cancel

- All three snipers (Aru, Kei, Hiyori) hold their shot on a **1 s charge** (locked in place). Holding sprint cancels the charge and fires early — but never before a **0.5 s floor** (costs ½ a dodge's boost). So the shooter picks any release point in the **0.5–1 s** window, and the target always gets at least that much glint-to-bullet warning.
- **Online floating unlock:** against a human defender the 0.5 s floor counts from the moment their client *actually rendered* the glint — the defender's client acks the glint's first frame and the server slides the earliest release to that ack + 0.5 s — the defender's half-second of SEEN warning is absolute. If the ack never arrives (defender's tab backgrounded, client stalled; the server waits up to 0.5 s), the earliest release becomes press + 1.0 s — exactly a normal full charge, so the attacker's worst case is simply losing the fast cancel for that shot. Offline play and bot defenders (who see server truth instantly) are unchanged.

**Sniper timing at a glance** (worked example: defender's network delay 0.05 s each way; projectile flight and 16 ms tick rounding excluded). Column definitions — *release time*: server clock, from processing the attacker's FIRE input to creating the projectile; *glint duration*: on each player's own display, from the frame the glint is first drawn to the frame the shot is drawn; *read & decide*: on the defender's side, from the glint's first drawn frame to the latest DODGE press that still reaches the server before the projectile exists.

| Release time | Glint duration (attacker's screen) | Glint duration (defender's screen) | Read & decide time |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0.60 (earliest the server permits) | 0.60 | 0.60 | **0.50** |
| 0.70 | 0.70 | 0.70 | 0.60 |
| 0.80 | 0.80 | 0.80 | 0.70 |
| 0.90 | 0.90 | 0.90 | 0.80 |
| 1.00 (server auto-fires) | 1.00 | 1.00 | **0.90** |

Three properties the table encodes: glint duration equals the release time on **every** screen (both endpoints of the interval shift by the same delivery delay, so its length is preserved for any observer); the defender's read & decide time is always the release time minus their round trip (one delivery lost at each end); and the earliest-release fence sits at ack + 0.5 s precisely so the read & decide column can never fall below 0.50 — the guarantee is produced by *placing the fence*, not by adjusting any clock. The full charge is the one release with no fence involvement: it fires at press + 1.0 s flat, so its read & decide time shrinks with the defender's round trip (lag-taxed like every ordinary attack), while the fast cancel's 0.50 is lag-proof.
- The **dodge** is the counter: a step grants **0.3 s** of i-frame immunity, so a well-timed dodge passes through the shot. Aru's and Hiyori's bullet speed is **4500 u/s** (b1105fd, user 2026-11-10, 4000 before, 2500 until 2026-10-22: near-hitscan — 0.044 s of flight at their 200 lock range, under three ticks); Kei's beam is instant.

### Aru (Unit 3) & Hiyori (Unit 19) — range zones & the lock reticle

- Damage is tiered by distance, **locked at fire time**: under 15 units → **20**, 15–50 → **35**, beyond 50 → **50**. Rushing a sniper is real counterplay; long range stays lethal. Hiyori's NTW-20 is tiered on the same zones: **20 / 40 / 60**.
- The lock reticle shows the current zone — plain brackets (<15), **+ cross ticks** (15–50), **+ inner bars** (50+). It appears both when *you* play Aru or Hiyori (your tier on the target) and when your lock target *is* one of them (which of her zones you're standing in).
- The reticle turns **red** not just when your target fires, but for the whole time a sniper (Aru **or** Kei) is **mid-charge with you as the target** — a continuous danger signal from glint to shot. Kei's live sweep channel also holds it red for the whole channel, whoever it is aimed at — the beam can hit anyone.

### Kei (Unit 6) — Railgun (照射ビーム beam)

- Fires an instant **hitscan beam** (30 damage, one hit per enemy per beam, blocked by walls, ~0.5 s fade) instead of a bullet. The beam also **deletes projectiles** it touches. The hit volume stops one combined body-width (beam radius + target radius) short of the wall it ends on (2026-09-10), so neither the quick beam nor the sweep channel registers on a unit standing just behind cover — before, the end cap reached up to ~3.2 u (channel ~4 u) through the wall.
- Holding the charge to the full **1 s** fires a **sweep channel**: a 1 s locked, steerable beam (1.5× width, **20 damage**, one hit per enemy for the whole channel). The stick steers it — horizontal and vertical — at ~10°/s; sprint cancels the channel. The fire cooldown is paused during the channel and starts when it ends.
- Her glint grows toward **2×** size as the charge fills, telegraphing a full-charge sweep.

**Bots vs. the sniper.**
- **As the shooter:** every sniper bot flips a **50/50 coin** per shot — release at the **0.5 s floor** (a fast snap; for Kei, the quick beam) or hold to the **full 1 s charge** (for Kei, the sweep channel). No in-between releases.
- **On defense:** when a glint aimed at it appears — from **any** enemy, locked or not (mirroring the human's edge-indicator awareness; earliest active charge wins) — the bot **rolls its reaction per charge**, and the slow roll is charger-aware:
  - **Anti-Aru** (the bullet snipers, Aru and Hiyori): **50% at 0.4 s** (i-frames open ahead of the earliest possible cancel, covering **every floor snap at any range** — but a full hold sails in after they end) / **50% at 0.8 s** (deliberately late: a snap lands first and cancels the pending dodge, but the i-frames ~0.8–1.1 s sit exactly on the **full hold's** impact). Against the shooter's 50/50 snap/hold flip neither side can be read; equilibrium **~50% of charges convert** (snaps beat slow rolls, holds beat fast rolls).
  - **Anti-Kei** (beam snipers): **50% at 0.4 s** (covers the instant quick beam) / **50% at 0.9 s** — the dodge starts just ahead of the **sweep channel's** aimed opening (i-frames ~0.9–1.2 s blanket it) and the follow-up sprint outruns the beam's steering at normal fighting ranges. (An 0.8 s roll would be dead weight here: the quick beam pre-empts it and the sweep outlives it.)

  Either way it's one dodge (0.3 s i-frames) plus a **1 s** committed sprint (1.5 s from 2026-11-01, 0.52 s before), both perpendicular to that sniper's line of fire, the sprint staying so every tick (it circles the sniper); lock and return fire stay on the current target throughout. Mid-charge hits still cancel a pending dodge, and a cooldown- or boost-blocked defender still eats the shot. After the committed sprint expires the bot has no awareness of a still-live sweep; the 1.5 s sprint (2026-11-01 to 2026-11-04) outlasted the sweep channel, so it mattered only when cover ended the sprint early; the 1 s one since is not measured against it.

**Bot trigger discipline.** A bot fires in continuous bursts of a fixed per-unit length (`botFireCap` — the "fire cap"), resting ~0.8–1.5 s between bursts. This rhythm is the bottom layer; the **bloom gate** (Bot logic below, 2026-09-22) sits on top of it and only shapes the machine guns' and the marksman rifles' fire inside their bands. Shots inside a burst pace at the weapon's own RPM-derived cooldown, so retuning a fire rate retunes the bot with it. Every auto's cap equals its **full magazine** — an auto bot fires until the mag runs dry and rolls straight into the reload; the shell-loading shotguns are burst-gated at 4 blasts per pull (Rabu empties her 10-shell box in one); the snipers run their charge cycle instead of bursting.

| Unit | Fire cap | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| Saori / Subaru / Minori | 30 | full mag |
| Asuna | 25 | full mag |
| Atsuko / Yuuka | 30 | full mag |
| Marina | 71 | full drum |
| Kurumi | 25 | full mag |
| Hoshino / Haruka | 4 | 4 blasts per trigger pull |
| Rabu | 10 | full box magazine |
| Koyuki | 100 | full mag |
| Hina | 150 | full drum (~7.2 s of continuous fire) |
| Maki | 100 | full mag (~12.7 s of continuous fire) |
| Fubuki | 30 | full mag |
| Aris | 4 | legacy formula (half mag) |
| Iori | 5 | full mag |
| Aru / Kei / Hiyori | — | charge cycle, no bursts |

A burst ends early if the mag runs dry (straight into the reload), if line of sight breaks (re-checked every 0.22 s; the burst then restarts from full), or if the target is **spawn-immune** — bots hold fire at immune targets and wake the moment immunity lapses. A bot's OWN spawn immunity does **not** hold its fire: a freshly spawned bot shoots from behind its protection window, same as a player would.

### Fubuki (Unit 10), Iori (Unit 20) & Aru (Unit 3) — ribbon tracers

Fubuki's Ruger rounds — and Iori's KAR98K since b1105dd — draw a **camera-facing ribbon trail** (2026-09-19, the demo line's marksman-rifle treatment): a real quad of world half-width 0.06 that re-aims at the camera every frame — live and while fading — so it reads as a thicker, smoke-like streak that thins with range like a physical object. Aru's PSG1 rounds carry the same ribbon at the demo line's deliberately fatter sniper width, half-width 0.10, fading over the sniper's 1 s — since 2026-10-15 the rifle's tail stays at the muzzle for 1 s (the whole flight) and fades over 1 s, the snipers' over 1.5 s — (2026-09-22, owner: "use the 0.8.3 trails" — with that the BA line's trails match the demo line gun for gun: the six autos on the 1-pixel line, the rifle on the 0.06 ribbon, the sniper on the 0.10 ribbon). **Both ribbons glow** (2026-09-23, owner pick "C" from four in-game samples): the core at the gun's own width sits over a second quad 3.2× wider and much fainter as a soft edge; the fade scales both quads. The samples were additive amber; the owner then asked for the autos' per-map ink (2026-09-23) and, because additive light can never match an alpha-blended line (it read white on the dark maps and as a pale highlight on the bright ones, where the autos' line is a dark stroke), finally for the same **visual** colour (2026-09-24): both quads now blend normally in the autos' ink — light grey `0xbbbbbb` on the dark maps, dark slate `0x3a3f4a` on Lobby, Airport and the Shooting Range — with the core's alpha set so that core over halo composites to exactly the line's 0.55. The streak reads as the same grey as the autos' line, wider and soft-edged. The cost is one extra quad (one draw call) per ribbon trail and the same per-frame re-aim the ribbon already did — no post-processing, nothing on the network. It runs on every map: the three bright-ground maps (Lobby, Airport, Shooting Range) were first left on the plain slate ribbon because additive light cannot darken, but the owner chose the same additive glow there too (2026-09-23, seen beside four normal-blend alternatives). The automatics carry **no trail at all** since 2026-10-14 (owner: the smoky streak stays on the semi-automatic rifles and the snipers — the unit data's `bulletTrail` flag, set in both the client's and the shared copy because online rounds are built from the shared sim data; 2026-10-15, after Fubuki's online trail went missing with the flag in the client copy only); shotgun pellets have none, Aris's bolt is its own trail and Kei's beam is hitscan; Hiyori's rounds take the snipers' 1.5 s fade on the plain 1-pixel line. Since the same day **every single-round gun flies the same spindle tracer** — the sniper's, at six times its old length (20.4 u) and a 0.15 u mid radius (was 3.4 / 0.18, with the autos at half size), and since b1105ek (2026-11-09) 1.8 times that length and 1.5 times that width, 36.72 u and 0.225 u, so the faster rounds stay visible — its body clipped to the distance flown so it grows out of the muzzle and never reaches back over the shooter; the shotgun pellets stay small spheres and the laser bolt its cylinder. Online (2026-10-15, owner: "a circle-like bullet visual near the firing character") the client flies each bullet on along its last snapshot velocity between snapshots (capped at 300 ms), and measures the spindle's body and the trail's start from the shooter's muzzle — the shooter's position in the same snapshot plus the sim's muzzle height, since a normal round's snapshot carries no spawn point — instead of from where the client first saw the round; measured from there the spindle began as a 1 u stub, a 0.3 u disc seen from behind, for one frame on every shot. The first frame now draws the distance already flown, like the offline first frame. The local player's **own rounds go further** (2026-10-15, owner: "the bullet visual spawns away from the firing character, it gives a laggy feel"): the client's prediction already fires them at the muzzle the moment the key goes down, so their spindle and trail are drawn from there at once and fly on, dying on walls and decks like an offline round; the server's copy of the round is never drawn on the shooter's screen: the local round keeps flying its own line to its own end — wall, deck, the drawn enemy's hit capsule, or the copy's going — and, since the **shared spread seed** (2026-10-15, owner: "implement seed"), on the very line the server's round flies: the server draws one random number per match and sends it once in `match:start`, and both the server and the client's prediction sample each round's direction inside its cone from a stream keyed by that seed, the fighter and the shot number (shared `spawnProjectiles`; shotgun volleys the same). The distribution is unchanged and a fresh seed every match keeps matches apart; nothing rides in the snapshots. What remains between the shooter's picture and the judgement is the enemy's movement over one round trip in the aim (0 standing, about 1 u walking, 3 u sprinting), against a hit capsule 1.6 u in radius. A first cut handed the mesh over to the copy and slid it onto the server's line, which read as the bullet swerving mid-flight (owner 2026-10-15). Hits, damage and what the other players see are the server's as before; a local round the server never fired is dropped once the server has run four inputs past it (or after a second); shotgun volleys stay on the server path. Because the prediction's clock is re-based on every snapshot, a late snapshot can make the forward ticks fire a held trigger's round a second time (owner 2026-10-15: "two parallel trails"), so the sim now counts trigger pulls per fighter (`shotsFired`) and stamps each round with its number (`shot`): a predicted round with the number of a local round already drawn is the same shot and is not drawn, the server's copy claims exactly the local round with its number, and a local round whose number the server has fired past without its copy showing is dropped (a plain time window let re-fires through — a protocol model of the prediction showed two to seven ghost rounds per three seconds of held fire at a 100 to 240 ms round trip — and the magazine count cannot serve where autoReload refills it between shots); rounds the replay fires that the forward ticks never did are drawn back-dated. A local round that dies on a wall before the server's copy shows (any wall within a round trip's flight, 60 u at a 100 ms round trip — the tally from the owner's match read 97 copies, 9 claimed, 88 drawn fresh) leaves a record, and the copy bearing its number claims the record and is never drawn, instead of flying the same shot a second time from mid-air. The client prints a tally to the browser console every five seconds while rounds are in flight (`[own-shots <build tag>] …`), and shows its build tag in the tab title. Visual only — hit detection is untouched.

## Bot logic

One bot brain drives every bot — all maps, all modes, offline and online (one module since 2026-10-17, `shared/src/sim/nbrain.js`: the server's `tickBot` and the offline client's `updateEnemy` each build a small host and call `nbBrainTick` once a tick, exactly as the Sudden Death brain is called; every bot slot in 1v1 / 2v2 / Trio / spectator uses the identical body). In **Sudden Death** the cover brain described in that section replaces Pursue / Engage; everything below is the Health Point brain.

**State machine** — `Defense > Maze > Engage > Pursue`, re-evaluated every tick:

- **Pursue** — closes to (or backs off to) the fighting band, **lock range ± 7**. Sprints in **latched legs** (2026-09-29): a leg arms only from a **full tank (250)** and runs down to **200**, then the bot walks until the tank is full again — roughly 0.7 s of sprint per 0.7 s of walk, and Maze routing shares the latch. Both bounds are times the map's boost scale: on District and Compound a leg runs from **1,250** down to **1,000**, about 3.6 s of sprint per 0.7 s of walk. (Both bounds used to sit on the 250 reserve: in Pursue the release won at exactly 250, so it never sprinted; in Maze the dispatch floor let one dash tick through every half second — the flickering sprint pose.) Elevation aids: jump up toward a higher target when close, hop off ledges toward a lower one, and on low ground hop onto any mountable ledge it brushes within jump reach.
- **Engage** — the in-band fight: orbits the target with a range correction toward the sweet spot, flips orbit direction to stay inside sight, and runs the peek-cover rhythm (tuck behind cover while the weapon cycles, drift out exactly when it's ready to fire). Two consecutive ticks of driving into a wall also flip the orbit — the tangent is perpendicular to the aim line, so reversing it always points away from the face just hit, and the bot unsticks itself inside the fight instead of breaking off to re-route. Opportunistically hops onto reachable ledges — high ground is the better vantage.
- **Defense** — triggered by a fresh hit (read off `lastHitAt`, which every landed hit stamps whether or not it stuns — until 2026-10-23 the brain read the stun window rising, so a `stunImmune` unit's bot never knew it was hit, and a hit landing inside a running stun of the same weight went unseen; the references were re-frozen at b1023n): a committed cover-sprint across the locked target's line (may spend boost to the hard floor — survival overrides the reserve — then walks on until the gauge is back over 50 before it sprints again: the escape sprint latch, b1104b), then a tuck-and-peek once cover breaks line of sight. The commit, by trigger (user 2026-11-04, "Change these four from 1500ms to 1000ms." — a knob each in `nbrain.js`, `BOT_DEFENSE_*_MS`; 1,500 ms from 2026-11-01, user: "change these three to 1,500 ms (keep their categories)"; 350, 600 and 520 ms before that): **1,000 ms** after a hit, after a hit while the locked enemy's sniper charges, and on after the anti-glint dodge's step. The hit's 350 ms evade window re-arms the commit every tick, so one hit's Defense runs about **1.35 s** on open ground (1.84 s at 1,500 ms, 0.69 s before that). The run **stays across the line** every tick (user 2026-11-01, "all staying vertical"): perpendicular to the line from the locked target, the same way round as it was running, so the bot circles it at the distance it was hit (within a metre on open ground) instead of flying off along a tangent — straight, the run had drifted up to 28 degrees off and some 13 u out. The old sustained-fire re-align (every 400 ms under fire, with a pull toward the band) gave way to it, so a bot hit far out circles there instead of closing as it weaves; a wall slide keeps its first 400 ms, and then the run turns back across the line the way round that leaves the wall (with both ways blocked it keeps the slide's line — before this, a bot under fire could stay pinned to the wall it had slid off). Bot duels on Plain Field, at its own boost scale of 1, stay about as close as before (mean separation 58 u straight, 54 u circling; Defense 92% and 93% of the time). At five times the boost — Plain Field's too for part of 2026-11-01 — the straight run, on the bigger gauge, had carried them far apart, and circling closed them up: 106 u to 50 u, Defense 78% to 90%. Reaching cover ends it early, through the tuck (300 ms) and the peek (until sight and a ready gun, at most 1 s). **Cover** (b1102o, user 2026-11-03: "the BOT just stood there and take consist damages") is the Sudden Death brain's silhouette test, one function both brains call (`sdbrain.js` `silhouetteSeenFrom`): a spot is cover when the locked target's muzzle reaches none of the capsule's front, shoulders, top and four shoulder corners, centred at the muzzle line's height, along the host's round line. It was the bot's eye line (1.6 over the body, under its own chest at 2.35 and the muzzle at 3.15, through the body's middle only), so low cover and an exposed flank counted as cover while rounds kept landing. **A hit in cover is no cover**: a round landing on a tucked or peeking bot drops the tuck and the rest of that Defense runs as the escape sprint, its cover broken (`botDefenseCoverBroken`, reset with the episode's other fields at the next Defense). Measured in bot matches (5 maps, no snipers, 60 s each): rounds landing on a tucked or peeking bot 2,766 to 407, tucks 1,367 to 735, a bot standing 0.6 s under three or more hits 12 times to once. The dodge's follow-through runs 1 s past the step (1.5 s from 2026-11-01 to 2026-11-04), circling the glint's attacker the same way, with the Sudden Death brain on too (its legs are the plain Defense's for that time), and under a Hide order it ends through the same tuck and peek: or at the full 1 s in the open (about 1 s when the unit dodged from cover was measured at the 1.5 s commit). The reference enemy is one life of it: if it dies and respawns mid-run, the locked target takes over, online and offline alike. A stuck-triggered commit (`BOT_DEFENSE_STUCK_MS`) is vestigial: a stuck bot goes to the Maze, and a stuck entry under fire takes the hit's commit on its first tick. A jumpable ledge lying **dead ahead** on the run's heading (within ~37°) is hopped without breaking stride — never while a sniper dodge is scheduled (airborne can't dodge), and funded at the flat **mandated-jump tier (60 boost**, 2026-08-22 — shared with command-mode route jumps; discretionary travel jumps fund from the 200 travel floor since 2026-09-29, 250 before; both times the map's boost scale, 300 and 1,000 on District and Compound**)**. If the escape wedges anyway, a wider-angle vault, then a direction flip.
- **Maze** — the router. Asks the nav grid for a real walk route (waypoint-followed, cut at the first spot that can already fire) and retries it on every stall signal; when no route exists it keeps moving on a minimal steer biased away from the last spot it got pinned at (a short back-out frees a truly wedged body first). Entered **proactively**: the moment the straight walk toward an out-of-band target is blocked (instantly when sightless; after 0.25 s when the target is visible over a low obstacle), plus the classic stuck detectors (a 1 s wedged/spinning window, a 1.5 s no-progress clock) and a 2 s no-line-of-sight clock. "Wedged" is judged on **net** movement — under 1.7 units in a second is stuck no matter how much wall the bot rubbed getting nowhere, which is what catches a slow slide along a face rather than only a dead stop.
  **Same floor** (b1102s, 2026-11-03; user, of Compound with the bot right under them on the big building's second floor: "the unit cannot break through a back and forth loop to get LOS or re-positioning"): `BOT_SAME_FLOOR_RISE` (2.5, `nbrain.js`) is the one test of "the same floor" — two heights under it apart are one floor (a kerb, a step), more another storey — asked by the vertical stack, the regained sight, the in-band arrival and now the final waypoint's arrival, which had read "within 3 units of the route's end" in the plan alone. The route up the stair to a firing spot on the floor above passes right under that spot, so the bot "arrived" on the ground floor, dropped the route, beelined back under its enemy and planned the same route again, back and forth, then re-planned every tick on the spot; now it arrives only on its destination's floor and walks on up the stair (on the second floor 7.7 s after setting out, in the fight 3 s later).
  **The cut's floor** (b1105ge, PT 2026-11-11; here 2026-11-12 with build b1105gf; user, of Compound's stair building with their unit on its upper deck: "When player unit is at the position, BOT got stuck beneath it."): the cut tries a sample every 6 units of the route, and a sample stands on the floor the route walks there — at a waypoint the waypoint's own floor, between two the floor a body walking the leg from its start stands on (`walkFloorTo`, `physics.js`: the rule the grid's walk edges are made by). It had stood on the topmost surface over the sample, so on a floor under a deck, a roof or a bridge the test looked from on top of it: from the walkway overhead a ground-floor sample "saw" a player on the deck, the route was cut before its stairs, every later plan at its first sample — no route — and the routeless steer walked the bot at the player's place on the map, the spot beneath them, for good. Not a matter of how many levels (asked: "is it because i only considered two levels and in this case there are more than 2 levels?"): the route search and the same-floor tests take any number, this test took none, and one floor over another is enough — Streets' bridge over its street is one.

**Cover Defense — measured and shelved (2026-09-27).** The owner asked whether Defense should take cover into account: a *Cover Defense* that, on a fresh hit, picks a spot hidden from **both** enemies (8 headings × 6 / 12 u rings, straight-walkable, scored cover-from-both > cover-from-whoever-sees-me-now > any, plus crossing the aim line; reach budgeted by boost — 80+ for the 12 u ring, 45+ for 6 u), sprints there committed until arrival (cap 1.2 s, no 400 ms re-perpendicularization on the way), counts as *in cover* only when hidden from every live enemy, keeps Defense's tuck-and-peek, re-picks when the second enemy regains sight and lands a hit, and spends to the same 8-boost floor. It was built on a scratchpad copy of the shared sim and measured against plain Defense in 2v2 bot duels on Factory, Streets and Airport (40 runs per cell, 45 s cap, Saori and Koyuki as the observed unit, both under a standing move order and in full autonomy): **worse in every cell** — mean survival 30.1 → 22.4 s, survived 38% → 12%, wins 38% → 11%, damage dealt down a fifth (enemy HP lost 156 → 125). The mechanism itself worked (most picks were double-cover spots, over 90% reached, no run hit the cap), so the loss is the behaviour: the unit takes about the same 16 hits per life, only faster — the straight run to cover is more predictable than Defense's 400 ms re-perpendicularized strafe, the 300 ms tuck is a standing target, the run points the gun away or out of sight, and two routing enemies turn a re-used cover spot into a flank. Same verdict as the hit-triggered Cover Hide (see Command mode): in this sim, sprinting across the aim line while firing back beats running to cover. Not adopted — nothing shipped, Defense unchanged, the Cover Hide plan left as it stands. Two variants were left untried should it be revisited: the 6 u ring only, and no tuck (peek and fire on arrival).

**Steering** — each tick, a moving bot's direction is the sum of two pulls: "toward where it's going" plus "away from any wall it's about to touch"; the second pull is what makes bots slide around obstacles instead of walking into them. Invisible unit-only fences (the kind bullets fly through) get sorted by height: a fence low enough to jump (Station's / Flashpoint's 4-high platform edges) gives **no push at all**, so the bot can walk right up to it and the hop is fired by the jump reflexes above; a fence too tall to jump (Square's 14-high fountain colonnade, the tall panes flanking Streets' bridge slopes) pushes like any solid wall. And a fence the bot is already standing **above** pushes nothing — it passes over freely.

**What counts as a ledge, and when a hop goes** (user 2026-10-24; one test shared by every perch behaviour): the top has to sit **1.7–4.8** above the bot's floor — lower is walked onto, higher needs a ramp — the lip must be unfenced, and the top must be at least **6 wide** (a body and a half; a jump carries 12–17 units horizontally, so anything thinner gets sailed clean over instead of mounted). Then the hop asks three questions, in every state: is the ledge **the way I am going** (its direction within ~60° of this tick's move — the route's next leg in Maze, the pursuit or orbit line otherwise), **has the enemy not fired at me in the last second** (a body in the air cannot dodge, so a hop under fire hands over a round), and — when the trigger is an enemy standing a jump higher — **is a ledge actually within reach** (1.1 u), not a blind jump at a belt ten units off. The Maze's width-0 exemption of 2026-08-10 (a strip too thin to be a vantage was still a legitimate thing to climb — bought with one Factory belt measurement) is withdrawn: on Warehouse it read the 0.25 m lips of the containers as steps. The Defense **vault** — pressed against a ledge with nowhere else to go — keeps its own gates; it is the way out, not a vantage, and jumping *down* off high ground is untouched. Measured on the shared sim (12 bot-vs-bot matches a map, 60 s each): hops a bot-minute Warehouse 18.5 → 7.5, Station 57 → 24, Town 38 → 19, Factory 2 8.3 → 6.5, Dust 13 → 13.5 (its jumps are descents); same-floor bounces — a hop that landed where it started — Warehouse 42 → 6, Town 82 → 5; Station's 4 m platforms are still climbed (131 → 57 climbs in the sample, the bounces gone). The three questions are three comparisons on numbers the brain already holds: no new scan, the brain's cost is unchanged.

**Sight** — a bot "sees" its target only along lines a bullet could actually fly: the sight ray is blocked by every solid obstacle, and by walkable **ramps and elevated decks** too — a ramp is solid fill, so nothing sees through the wedge; a bridge deck on open pillars blocks only rays that cross the deck plane, so two units both *under* the bridge still see each other. The eye sits **1.6 above the body centre** (`BOT_LOS_EYE_HEIGHT`) on both hosts; until 2026-10-17 the offline client read it from the root + 1.6, 2.35 higher than online, so offline bots saw over cover that online bots did not.

**Seeing vs shooting** — those are two separate questions, asked from two different heights. *Sight* is the eye line, and it drives awareness: target choice, routing, cover, when to break off. *Firing* is gated on the **muzzle** line instead — the exact line the bullet will take, tested with the bullet's own rules. The two are not interchangeable: a bullet leaves the chest, not the eye, so anything sitting in the gap between them (a slope's guard rail, a deck's underside, the slope surface itself) used to let a bot hold a picture-perfect sight line into a shot that died on the geometry. The gate now *is* the shot test, so a bot that pulls the trigger always has a path — and it will also take a shot through an invisible unit-only fence, because bullets genuinely pass through those. Sight treats such a fence as opaque only where it is flagged `blocksBotSight` (Streets' under-slope bars and slope gates — see-through walls the bot must route around); every other unit-only fence is as transparent to sight as it is to bullets.

**Sniper play** — covered in the sniper section above: 50/50 snap-or-hold as the shooter; as the defender, a per-charge dodge roll timed against the charger's kit, one committed perpendicular dodge + sprint (the sprint 1,000 ms since 2026-11-04 — 1,500 ms from 2026-11-01, 520 ms before).

**Bloom gate and suppressing fire** (ported from the demo line 2026-09-22) — a bot fires freely only while its target sits inside the gun's *current* **gate line**, re-checked every tick from the live distance: one closed-form division, run before the obstacle scan so an out-of-range poll costs nothing. The line is a fixed width divided by the bloomed cone: for the **auto weapons it is 8.84 ÷ cone — the distance where the cone still lands one round in three on a standing target** (the 3.2 × 6.4 capsule is a third of the cone's disc at radius 4.42; 2.76× the sure-hit line); for the **marksman rifles (Fubuki, Aris) it is the sure-hit line itself, 3.2 ÷ cone** (`botGateWidth: 3.2`); **the machine guns Koyuki and Maki have no line at all** (`botGateWidth: 0`, owner 2026-09-25; Hina had none either until b1105fe, user 2026-11-10: "Give Hina the same 33% hit rate gating other auto-weapons have", rode the autos' 8.84 until b1105ga, and since then, user 2026-11-11: "change hina hit rate gating from 33% to 20%", gates on a line of her own, `botGateWidth: 11.42`, where her cone still lands one round in five — a 0% threshold in both modes: they spray on the burst / rest rhythm alone, and for them a burst is the whole magazine — `botFireCap` equals the mag for every auto — so the reload is the rest). At their caps the other autos' lines sit at 147 (Saori, Asuna), 110 (Atsuko) and 80 (Marina), so inside the rifles' and SMGs' 43–63 bands the gate never trips and what you see there is the plain **burst / rest rhythm** of the trigger discipline above. When a line does close on a target (an auto pushed out past its capped line), the bot **holds until the cone has fully recovered** (200 ms + the bloom ÷ recovery) — the line drifting back out past a standing target does not release the hold, so there is no one-round trickle — and opens up again from the base cone. A target that **closes in** past where the line stood when the hold began releases it at once. Beyond the base cone's line a recovered auto fired a committed **suppress burst** (five rounds for the rifles and machine guns, ten for the SMGs) and repeated, until b1105fw set every base cone but Aris's to 0: the line of a 0 cone has no end (it stood at 442 for the 0.02 guns, 295 for Atsuko, 221 for Hina, 147 for Marina), so no auto fires one now; the rule stays for any gun with a base cone. The **marksman rifles** live on the gate: each shot adds 0.10 of bloom and 0.17/s takes 0.59 s to shed it, so at their band they settle at one shot per ~0.6 s; their suppress burst is a single round (`botSuppressBurst: 1`), and only Aris, her 0.02 base kept, still has a line, 160, to fire it past. Shotguns (fixed pattern) and the snipers (their cone out-reaches lock range) are never gated. Offline and online run the same rule (shared `bloom.js`, `botMayFire` / `botGateDistance`). The demo line measured the gate line's effect over 6,336 bot duels (see that branch's README); this line has not been re-measured.

**Bot fire-gate thresholds** — the hit rate on a standing target below which the bot stops firing (the line is *width ÷ current cone*; 33% = width 8.84, 100% = the 3.2 sure-hit width, 0% = no line):

| Unit | 1v1 | 2v2 |
|---|---|---|
| Saori, Asuna, Subaru, Minori, Atsuko, Marina, Yuuka, Kurumi | 33% | 33% |
| Hina | 20% | 20% |
| Koyuki, Maki | 0% (no gate) | 0% (no gate) |
| Fubuki, Aris, Iori | 100% | 100% |
| Hoshino, Haruka, Rabu, Aru, Kei, Hiyori | never gated | never gated |

**Reload hide** (the 2026-08-08 cover reload, re-based on the Hide stance 2026-09-26) — weapons with a manual reload of 3 s or more (Hina's and Koyuki's 5 s) spend it in the **Hide** stance: the unit enters Hide the tick the reload starts and leaves it the tick the reload completes, with every Hide rule — out of every enemy's sight, pacing its cover, slipping away from a closing enemy, no Defense on a hit, firing back only from where it stands. Every bot does it (enemies too); on your own units in command mode the shield badge shows, entering **wipes a standing force lock**, and a new order during the reload releases the hide for the rest of that reload, exactly as it would any Hide (a Hide you set yourself is not released by the reload ending). **A unit under a move order keeps it**: its reload runs as a *Cover Hide* bound to the order instead (2026-09-26 — cover inside the order's area first, for the whole reload, no badge), and the unit resumes its post when the reload completes; a new move order landing mid-reload takes over at once. Per-shell and auto reloaders (Hoshino, Haruka, Aris) are excluded by design — they're always mid-reload, so they'd never come out. The counterplay is to push: hiding beats a stationary opponent, not a committed one.

**Station's hops, and Factory's belts** (b1024z10–z11, user 2026-10-24) — Station: a bot that had left a platform — walked off its edge while orbiting, or jumped down to close on the enemy — hopped straight back onto it, the platform being the strong ground the low-ground rule reached for, and landed at whatever range that gave. Gone on the user's call (`nbrain.js`): the **low-ground hop** — "on low ground, take any reachable platform, no 'toward player' gate; on Station the decks are the strong positions" — in Pursue, Engage and Defense alike; and with it two things tried the same day and dropped the same day: a fourth question for a hop ("did I not just come down from there?", a 4 s refusal of a hop back to the floor just left) and a gate on the pursuit's deliberate drop (only when the enemy stands lower) — the question read heights, not places, so it also kept a body backing off from hopping onto the other platform, and a hop that keeps the range must stay. What remains climbs for a reason: the pursuit's climb toward an enemy a jump higher, the Maze's hop onto a ledge in its way, a route's vault at its link, Defense's sprint vault and its vault out of a wedge. Measured over six bot-minutes of 1v1: a body left a platform once or twice a minute (six or seven before) and never came straight back up the same side (five or six times before); it spends 9–29 % of a match on a platform, where it was 30–80 % — the platforms are climbed by routes and the pursuit only. And the wedge detectors read the sim's own note of a wall (b1024z12, user 2026-10-24: "the bot got stuck on the railway under fire"): Defense and Engage judged "I am pressed against a wall" from the steering's boxes alone, and the steering leaves jumpable fences out on purpose — Station's 1 m platform fronts among them — so a body pressed against a front stood still, its vault out of a wedge asleep, once the low-ground hop that used to lift it was gone (measured: 1.6–3.3 s a minute still under fire on the tracks, 71–90 % of a match down there). Now moving into the face physics pushed the body out of last tick (`host.wall`, `pushedInto` in `nbrain.js`) counts as wedged too, whatever the thing; Pursue's wedge was always read from movement and needed nothing. Measured after: 0.0–0.3 s a minute still under fire, 7–25 % of a match on the tracks, the platforms climbed again through the vault out of the wedge — so the quick return up the side a body fled from is back, two to five a minute, now as the escape's way out rather than an idle hop. Every jump a bot takes is tagged with its rule (`botHopWhy`: pursue-climb, pursue-descent, pursue-low, maze-perch, maze-route, low-ground, defense-ahead, defense-vault, cover-move) for the headless harness to read. Factory: no bot ever crossed a belt, by two faults of the nav grid (`navgrid.js`). A walk edge's fit at the crossing's midpoint was read at the floor the walk stands on there — past the step up onto the belt top (2.6 units, a step's height) — so the 2.9-unit lips before the step were never met, the belt read as walked onto, and the sim's lips then stopped the body; and a jump link between two walk components needed a rise of at least `JUMP_RISE_MIN`, which the belt's 2.6 falls short of. Now every walk edge is also swept at the lower floor's body height (`walkSegmentBlocked`), and an island link needs no least rise (the components are apart, so no walk joins them; a shortcut within one component keeps it). Factory gains 48 links, ground to belt top, and the bots vault its belts in the headless matches (about one crossing per bot-minute); elsewhere one walkable node changes (Town) and four routes grow by 2 m where a low thing really blocks the walk. The two equivalence tests' references are re-frozen at b1024z12 (`shared/tools/freeze_refs.py`).

**Where a perch is looked for (b1024z15).** The plain brain's two floor searches, `findHighGroundPerch` and the first loop of `findDescentDirection`, used to walk every floor of the map, up to five times a tick per bot; on Compound that is 16,878 floors, and it was the brains' largest cost there. They now ask the sim's point broadphase (`obstaclesNearPoint`) for the floors within the seek radius and test only those. The broadphase hands back a superset of those floors in the list's own order, so the same floor wins as before: the frozen-brain tests pass unchanged against the b1024z12 refs. In the headless harness Compound's mean bot cost fell from 0.65 to 0.29 ms per tick; the spikes, which are grid searches over 20k nodes, are untouched.

**The firing-position search's cost (b1025c, user 2026-10-25: the online lag on Compound).** Maze's first choice, the search for the nearest spot that sees the target from inside the band, was the brains' largest single cost on the big maps: it grew from the bot's feet in every direction with no destination (Dijkstra), and every candidate it reached was tested for sight against every box and every floor of the map — on Compound 22,666 and 16,878 — at two eye heights; three bots could run it in the same tick, and the server's 6 ms search budget covered only the hide search. Four rules now, in `nbrain.js` navPlan and `navgrid.js` findFiringPath: the sight test reads the broadphase like every other sight test (identical answers); the expansion is directed (A*: the key is the walk so far plus a lower bound of the walk still needed to reach the band — the straight-line distance to the band's edge in cells, over 1.5, so it never exceeds the true cost and the spot found is still a cheapest one; 300 random pairs per map: every spot that differed from the old search's tied it exactly in walking cost); the search gets the tick's slice (`host.deadline`) and parks when it runs out, continuing next tick from the same open set while the bot follows an interim route to the target (the walk path, cut at the first spot that fires); one route search per tick per match (`host.searchSlot`, the hide search's turn rule); and a bot farther than `BOT_FIRING_SEARCH_REACH` (2.5) times its band's upper edge walks first and searches only inside the reach — a shotgun beyond 117 u, an SMG beyond 142 u, while a sniper's reach is 317 u, so it keeps crossing the map to its lane. `smoothPath`'s per-chord prefilter, which walked every box of the map, is gone too (the overlap test reads the point index itself). Headless, Compound, four shotgun bots: 0.43 → 0.22 ms per tick, ticks over 16 ms 19 → 5 a minute; the 1v1 harness's worst tick 113 → 22 ms. The references were re-frozen at b1025c (the turn and reach rules change the bots' timing). **The walk route too (b1025e, user 2026-10-25: "the respawn lag is the far route").** The spikes left after b1025c all fell on ticks where a bot stood 200–470 u from its target — after a respawn, typically — and none within half a second of a death or a respawn itself: the walk route across the map (`findPathOnGrid`, the interim and fallback route) still ran whole in one tick. It is resumable now like the firing search (its job on the route record, the tick's slice, parked before a pop so the resumed search is the unbudgeted one step for step — `shared/test/route_budget.test.js`), a call always makes 32 pops of progress so a deadline already past cannot stall it, and navPlan runs one search a tick: the walk route first while the bot has no route at all, the firing search next, its spot replacing the interim route; until any route lands the Maze leg's fallback walks the bot toward the target. A plan still searching when the bot arrives at its route's end is kept rather than dropped. Headless Trio 2v2 with respawns on Compound, 90 s: ticks over 16 ms 7 → 2, the worst 49 → 19 ms, none within half a second of a death or respawn; the 1v1 harness's worst tick 22 ms; the four-shotgun match fires as before (196 shots). References re-frozen at b1025e. **The budget itself (b1025f, user 2026-10-25: "smoothness over the bots' route"):** the server's slice is 2 ms (6 before): with the searches resumable the slice is spent nearly every tick while any bot's search is pending, and on the free instance's tenth of a core 6 ms of wall clock was a third of the tick for a tenth of a fast core's progress — the tick ran late every time and the players' prediction snapped back (the "backtrace" report). A tick that starts half a tick late grants no search at all (`matchState.searchClosed`, `LATE_TICK_GAP_MS`), the bots' order rotates each tick so the one search a tick is shared instead of hogged by the first bot, and the server logs its tick cost and late starts every 30 s (`[tick] 30 s: …`). A cross-map route on Compound is ~90 ms of work on that core, ~45 ticks at 2 ms, ~0.7 s; a firing search from far ~0.85 s; three bots in turn stretch the last one's to about 2 s. The bot walks toward its target meanwhile, and a hit still puts it in Defense: the state machine runs every tick, the route record waits.

**The slide** (b1026k, 2026-10-29; found measuring Town's cost after the lips of b1026j) — the Sudden Death follower (`sdbrain.js`, THE ROUTE) dropped a route the moment the heading to the next waypoint pointed into the wall the body touched, marked the waypoint and searched again. Where the route's first square lay across a thin post's line — Town's stair balusters, a body pressed on one as it came onto the stairs from the side, the way the lips' new links sent it — the search returned the same route, dropped the same tick, every tick to the end of the match: 2,000 searches, 6.5 ms of sim a tick, the body never moving. A heading that only grazes the wall now goes on along it — the heading less its part into the wall, what the push-out leaves of it anyway — with the route kept; the route is dropped as before head-on (less than `SD.slideMin` of the heading, sin 20°, left along the wall) or when the slide at one waypoint has gone on for `SD.slideTicks` ticks (45, 0.7 s). Town's match after: 1.3 ms a tick and 100 searches (6.5 and 2,000 before); District's 1.6 with the slide on or off. The bots' equivalence tests are re-frozen at b1026k: play differs wherever a body brushed a wall on a route.

**Map-agnostic by design.** The bot rules that shipped carry no per-map special cases; a set of Station-only behaviors was built and field-tested, then deleted once repositioning Station's spawns solved the same problem more cleanly.

## Controls

| | |
|---|---|
| **Mobile** | On-screen joystick + buttons |
| **PC** | `WASD` move · `J` fire · `K` sprint · `L` dodge · `Space` jump · `U` switch target (2v2; cycles lanes on the Shooting Range and the watched unit in Spectator mode) · `Space` at a ladder's foot or exit mounts it, on the ladder lets go (see *The ladders* under Maps) |
| **Command mode** | Tap unit → tap map = move order (hold to pick the floor) · drag from unit = move order · tap enemy = force lock · select unit → tap the shield above its card = Hide (any new order releases it) · double-tap unit = clear · nothing selected: tap pinned enemy = drop your locks on it · drag ring = move the order (40 s restarts) · double-tap ring = drop move order · drag pan · pinch / wheel zoom · `Q`/`E` / right-drag / two-finger twist rotate · `V` toggle (offline) |

Double-tap `K` (or the sprint button) while holding a movement direction to lock sprint. The lock releases only after the stick/keys stay **neutral for a sustained 0.18 s** — flipping direction through the joystick center (left→right) or swapping movement keys keeps the locked sprint alive; letting go still stops it almost instantly. Dodge (step) grants 0.3 s of damage immunity (i-frames) — the full duration holds even when the dodge runs into a wall (the unit stops at the wall; the animation and i-frames don't cut short).

## HUD & unit displays

- **Overhead HP bar** above every unit (ported 2026-08-06): bright cyan `#7fe9ff` for the camera unit's team (2026-09-22, owner pick from in-game samples — settled on `#7fe9ff` after an hour on the paler `#a5f1ff`; the 2026-09-19 soft cyan read too dim, the near-white before it read as plain white), orange for its opponents — spectate-relative, so the watched unit's side always reads cyan. **Edged like the edge arrows** (2026-09-22, owner pick "E" from six in-game samples): a dark outline in the arrows' stroke colour `#0b1622`, ~2.9 px on the ~92 × 12 px on-screen bar (the arrow's stroke is ~2.7 px), rings the track pill, and the track is laid over a pill of the team ink so its empty part carries a faint cast of the team colour (its rounded corners keep a hairline of it) — the tint that set sample E apart from the plain-outline sample. The outline sits outside the pill, so the fill and track keep their old size; the old 2 px steel hairline is gone. **Portrait** (2026-09-26): the unit's picker thumbnail sits left of the bar in the same dark rounded frame (44 texture px, ~25 px on screen, the bar's outline width as its border), and the whole "portrait + bar" group stays centred over the unit's head with the bar at its old height — the portrait overhangs the bar evenly above and below, with a clear gap between its frame and the bar's outline. Constant on-screen size at any distance (compensated by true view depth) and visible through cover; teammates' bars ride the head at a fixed screen gap, the **locked** enemy's group sits just above the crosshair **as drawn right now** (2026-09-26, was a fixed 3 rs above the reticle's centre and read far too high): above the bracket square, or above Aru's mid / far tier marks when her reticle shows them, at the bracket's current bloom-grown size, and above the sniper-charge glint while one is showing — a screen-constant ~5–10 px gap, floored at the head anchor. Hidden while the command diorama runs (its info cards carry HP and stamina), and on the **Shooting Range** (owner 2026-09-28: no portrait, no bar on the lanes or the player).
- **Screen-edge triangles**: a triangle on the screen's edge points at a unit off the frame, in that unit's own colour, its corner chip's and its bar's: offline the role palette, online the server slot's (b1105ea and b1105eb, 2026-11-09; the friendly-bar cyan and a fixed orange before). Who has one: in 2v2 the teammate; the enemy that is not locked, since the locked one stays in the angled view's frame under its bracket; in **free pov**, which has no lock, every live enemy, the one locked before the switch too and the one enemy of a 1v1 as well (b1105fv, 2026-11-11); on a ladder every live enemy (b1105a, below). The floating chevrons that used to hover over both units in frame were removed on 2026-09-19 because they sat right on top of the overhead HP bars. Each triangle carries a glint halo, drawn over it, while its unit is a sniper mid-charge.
- **Lock bracket bloom** (2026-09-22): the crosshair brackets around your target are drawn at half their former base size (owner call, matching the demo line) and grow with your *own* current spread bloom on **one universal scale** — ×1 with no bloom, growing at the same rate on every unit up to a ceiling of ×4.5, reached at 0.19 of bloom (the reference is 0.38, the demo SVD's full cap, with the growth run at twice that rate). Saori at her 0.06 cap shows about ×1.74, Marina ×1.92, Koyuki at 0.12 ×2.84, Aris ×4.32, Fubuki pins at ×4.5 from her 4th quick shot on. The commander's lock-share triangles and the command-mode markers keep their size.
- **Corner HUD**: HP bars per team member with the Trio weapon rows underneath (the fielded weapon marked in gold); a bar is as long as its unit's HP over the 100 HP base (Kurumi's 1.2×; the overhead pill and the command card the same, 2026-10-24). Online the bars follow the server slot with a white rim on your own — see the Online section. In spectator mode the watched unit's corner bar wears a white glow rim.
- **Command mode** hides the joystick, the action buttons and the bottom-center stamina bar; the diorama's info cards show each unit's HP and stamina instead.
- **The ladders** (b1105a): while the player climbs, the Target / Fire / Dodge / Sprint buttons hide (the jump button stays — it lets go), the lock bracket hides, and a screen-edge arrow rides every live enemy, the locked one included, its glint as ever; the camera looks from the climber's back along the ladder. A spectator's buttons stay. In command mode a move order whose route climbs a ladder draws the climb solid amber on the dashed route preview, and holding the finger down cycles to the way without a ladder when there is one (b1105b).
- **The ally tactic** (b1105gq, b1105gr): in the shooter view of a 2v2 with a bot teammate, a small button the fullscreen button's size — right of the fullscreen button on a screen up to 720 px wide (b1105gs) and on one 1100 px wide or more, at the fullscreen button's mirror place right of PAUSE in between (b1105gt) — opens FORWARD (the teammate stands 30 nearer its target for 10 s) and BACK OFF (it hides for 10 s); while a call stands the button itself is lit, and the call's pill is lit when the two are opened again; a tap on the lit pill takes it back, a tap on the other replaces it. Hidden in command mode with the stick, in 1v1, beside a human teammate, in Sudden Death and on the Shooting Range; shown and inert in spectator mode. See the entry under Modes.

## Maps

Nineteen arenas: Plain Field, Streets, Factory, Scrapyard, Square, Lobby, Station, Flashpoint, Airport, Town, Spacecraft, Dust, Warehouse, Compound, Hospital, District, Neon, Backrooms, Backrooms (Anomaly). Every one of them is online; only the Shooting Range is offline (`offlineOnly` in the client's MAP_DATA hides it from the online picker — until 2026-10-22 a hand-kept list said which maps were online, and Dust and Warehouse had been left off it). Each has its own cover layout and elevation; Station has raised platforms players jump up onto, and Airport centers on a raised security plateau — glass-fenced rims, four ramp entrances, and a metal-detector checkpoint as the only way across the middle. On Streets, the storefront towers are solid to their full height (they block movement, fire, and bot sight) and their **rooftops are standable** — only flight gets up there, making them Aris's high ground. Streets' footbridge is **double width** and has two distinct spaces underneath, which behave differently: the lane under the **deck** is open ground — the deck stands on pillars, so you walk and shoot through it freely — while the wedge under each **slope** is solid. Nothing crosses a slope from below: no walking, no bot sight, no bullets. The four bridge approaches are screened by full-height **bastion hoardings** (8-tall panels on the deck at z ±16..±28 on both rail lines — true cover for the whole unit) that fade in step with the bridge itself. Each ramp foot is **road-blocked** (2026-10-02): one scaled-up concrete Jersey barrier 10 u out from the mouth on the street, 20 long, covering 20 of the 32-wide approach with a 2 u overlap past the centre line and a 12 u lane left open on the other side, so a unit coming straight at the ramp steps round it. The north barrier is the south one rotated 180° about the map centre. It stands to the vending machines' top (y 8, full cover: it blocks movement, fire and bot sight) and fades like the bridge when it comes between the camera and a unit. A second barrier at the plaza lip, making the pair a chicane, shipped first and was removed the same day: it blocked part of the ramp mouth itself. Chosen through sample rounds: a staggered pair over a side-by-side gate, cover height over a hop-able low barrier, and a plain barrier over striped, railed or two-tier dressings. The offline map list also carries the **Shooting Range** — the no-opponent practice map (target sliders, per-lane score screens) behind the pre-bloom hit-rate tables; the current tables above are measured in the shared sim; the map Random card never rolls it. On Streets the footbridge group (deck, slopes, rails, gates, pillars) fades whenever it hides **any living unit** from the camera — your ally included (2026-09-11), which is what keeps a teammate ordered under the deck visible in the command view.

**Town** (2026-10-18) — the first modelled map, cut from a glTF scene: *Invasion Map - miniroyale.io* by zct_33 on Sketchfab, licensed CC Attribution ([the model](https://sketchfab.com/3d-models/invasion-map-miniroyaleio)), served as `client/public/maps/invasion.glb` (13 MB, downloaded once per session from the map card's first tap). The scene is a town in a valley in metres; the characters are human-sized, so it is placed at **4 units per metre** (a 6.4-unit body is 1.6 m), and only the flat town is playable: **Town** is the block around the central square and its three-storey building, 135 × 100 m (540 × 400 units), with the ring of buildings and the streets, the raised terrace to the west and the plateau to the east. The spinning UFO is left out. The collision is generated, not modelled, by one rule: **what a body can stand on is floor; what stands in its way is a box on it.** `shared/tools/glb_collision.mjs` rasterises the scene's triangles into 0.25 m columns. A column may hold several floors: every stack of surfaces (surfaces within a step of each other are one floor: a kerb, a slab) with 2 m of surface-free air above it, so doorways, garages and the underpass are floor and their roofs are ceiling boxes; a floor sloping down from the street is read to 3 m below it (a sunken road or yard is a floor, the rims around it are its walls), while the model's hidden underside (terrain plates more than 0.12 m under the street) is not read at all. Which floor a body walks is decided by the flood: a road under a bridge and the bridge are two floors of one column, and where both are walked the upper one is a **slab** — a surface with a body reaching down only to the passage's roof — while a lone plane over a walked space (the plate under a raised yard) is the model's hidden interior and stands on the ground. What stands on a floor (a wall, a fence, a car's side — with whatever is above it that a body cannot fit under, so a car's sides and its roof are one thing) is a box on it; what hangs over it low enough to hit a body and has an edge to it (a gate arm, a bench) is a box in the air that a body hops; a lone plane hanging there is a ceiling. From the street the floor is flooded over steps of 0.65 m (the sim's step height, raised from 0.4 m to the model's tallest kerb, so a kerb is no obstacle and needs no collision of its own) and 45-degree rises, through columns a body passes: a **slope** is a sloped stretch in any direction (3–25°, the model's roads and ramps), at least three cells long and rising at least 0.15 m, one fitted plane — a `plane` surface, so a diagonal ramp is one surface and not a staircase of decks — skirted where its side stands more than a step above the floor beside it; a stretch steeper than that, up to 50°, is a **flight of steps**, and a plane too: a body climbs it smoothly, where treads at their own heights stepped it (a column's floor is the highest walked floor over it with no headroom beneath — a stair's treads over the hidden plate under them, a raised bed's top over its plate — so stairs and beds are floors, not things standing on the plate); a **deck** is any other raised or sunken floor, each stretch of cells within 6 cm of its first cell one region written at its own mean height — the terrace at 2.35–2.40 m is one floor, not a patchwork of 0.25 m classes with one-unit steps between them (a flat surface, a box beneath it); **low cover** is a thing a body hops onto from what it stands on beside it — the floor, or low cover it already hopped onto, so a car is climbed bonnet first, then roof — no more than 1.5 m per hop and 3 m in all (a thing wider than 3 m both ways, a plaza or a roof, is stepped onto from a floor only); everything else that stands is a **wall**, in 1 m height classes up to the 7 m flight ceiling so a merged box stands at most 1 m above the model's — except a thick thing no more than 6 m above the floor beside it (a kiosk, a garage, a truck, a tree's crown), which is **standable**: a floor on top at the thing's own height, for a body that lands on it from the air; buildings are taller, and nothing stands on a building. Any step between two walked floors up to 1.5 m (a kerb, a raised pavement's edge, a platform's) is climbed on the step itself: the high floor's first cell at the line is a ramp from the low floor to the high, and the high floor's body starts a cell further in, so a body below is never lifted before the line and a body above sinks into the floor only within the cell at its edge (a 45-degree ramp laid on the low floor, as long as the step was tall, stretched the walk beside a 1.5 m edge through the air) — the edges between the roads and the pavements are visual only; ground beyond such a step, a flat floor 2 m across and more than 9 m² (a raised pavement, a platform), is walked too, where an object's top that narrow (a car's bonnet, a hedge, a planter) is hopped, never walked. A kerb's own face is its floor's edge, not a thing standing on the road; a thing on a raised floor stands from that floor, so its box starts there and its height is its own; a post under a tree's crown is the post, not the crown. A thing no thicker than 0.35 m and no taller than 2 m over the floor beside it (a fence, a railing, a post) stops a body and passes rounds and sight; a slim thing taller than that (a bus's side, a wall's plane) is a wall. A slim run no taller than 3 m that rises from under the floor beside it with that same floor on both of its sides is a seam of the model's terrain tiles (their edges meeting under a street) and nothing stands there. A column no floor of which a body reaches, with nothing standing in it, is outside the model: narrower than 1.5 m it is a gap between the model's tiles (a 25 cm slot across the west terrace, which used to be a 2.35 m hole) and takes the floor beside it; wider, it is no floor and a 25 m wall — the world's edge, so a body on the terrace's north rim or the town's slab meets a wall where the model has nothing (it used to read as the street, and a body walked off into nothing). A flat face more than 12 cm under the street is the model's hidden plate — the ground plane around the town's slab, the plate under the whole map, the tiles' bottoms — and is not read, but a sloped face there is a ramp going down and is read however deep: the garage ramps beside the south buildings, which start 13 cm under the street and run 2 m down to the garage floors, used to be cut off by depth, so a body stood on air over them and then met the void's wall. A box with a floor on top lets a body pass once its feet are within a step of that top — the ground rule steps it onto the floor — so a half-metre step onto a deck is a step and not a wall. Equal-height columns merge into boxes, and only what touches the crop is written: Town 7278 boxes and 6554 floors. **Spawn places** (user 2026-10-18): six set by hand on the generator's json (`spawn_pick.mjs`, each on a metre of walkable floor each way at its own height — the grid's node already fits the body, and a ship's corridor is 3.5 m wide) — the homes on the west terrace (2.35 m) and the east terrace (3.9 m), and four more (the north-west terrace, the north street, the south-west street, the south-east terrace). A Trio respawn picks among all six (`shared/src/sim/spawn.js`, the one call the server and the offline client both make): with a living teammate, a place the teammate sees, the nearest to it; without one, a place no living enemy sees, the farthest from the nearest enemy — sight by the bots' own rule at eye height; a place within 8 units of a living body is passed over. Maps without `more` places keep the slot's own start. The served model is the original minus the billboards' logo (`glb_delogo.mjs`: the one material that carried it, flattened to the board's blue). In command mode the stand's plate and plinth sit under the map's lowest floor, as the model's streets run a few centimetres under its reference and its garages metres under. Whatever stands taller than a body beyond the arena's edge — the wall along the south edge (8.3 m) and the buildings beyond it, the houses past the west, north and east limits — goes translucent while it hides a unit from the camera: a body at an edge has the camera outside the arena, behind whatever stands there (the Streets buildings' rule on each thing's own hull, a rule of every modelled map; a hand-set box used to name the south-edge wall and a stretch of the buildings behind it, and the buildings east and west of the box still hid a body on the garage roofs — user 2026-10-19). Known costs: a round trip for the model on the first play; things hanging above head height (signs, awnings, tree canopies) are not boxes, so rounds and a flying Aris pass them as before. The flood starts from **the street** — the one connected street-level floor that is largest — not from every street-level plane the model has (2026-10-24): the plate inside the west pavement's kerb skin was a street-level floor too, so the sealed 0.9 m slot between the pavement's edge wall and its skin counted as a walked passage, the slab over it was left hollow, and a body that hopped the 0.4 m wall beside the pavement ramp fell 2.4 m in with no way out; the plaza's slot of 2026-10-22 was the same cavity, lidded by hand then. The lid is gone, the one rule covers both, and what the flood does not reach is solid; with it, a guard reads an edge by the sim's own box window — a thing whose top is less than 0.41 m over the floor and carries no top to stand on is walked over onto air, so the drop beyond it is to whatever lies under the thing (the slab's end over the filled slot read as level ground until then) — and a thing over columns no body walks stands from the ground (every modelled map regenerated; what a body can reach from its spawn is unchanged on Backrooms and Warehouse, Dust gains 11 m² of its east basin's floor, and Spacecraft's two sunken engine bays — 1.85 m pits a hop over their rim dropped a body into — are solid too). **Its collision remade (2026-10-29, the third day; user: "remake the Town c-boxes, the same simplified procedure District has, and don't change any visual")** — the model untouched, the boxes and floors read by District's parts generator in its PIECES mode (`glb_parts_collision.mjs`): the Invasion Map's 52 meshes are whole buildings, so each is split into its separable pieces (triangles joined by shared vertices, welded by position), a wall, a roof slab, a car's body each a box with its lid; the ground nodes (`gbase_`, `ground_`, the police station's) read face by face — a level face a floor with a lidded block under it to the street, a standing face a thin wall, a sloping one a plane, so the terraces are solid, their banks ramps, the kerbs steps; a ramp's level faces are its landings; the backdrop grass tiles and the UFO left out. 5,485 boxes and 6,161 surfaces (7,271 and 4,602 from the cell reading, 2,430 of those boxes under half a metre), 15,970 nodes in the grid (9,778) — and, the same day, the pieces that are no bricks read by their faces (THE SHAPES, tools README): the police station's garage open, the housing plot's yard and its cellar ramps where one box 2.75 m deep lay over all of it, the stairs' cheeks and handrails at their own heights, every flight of treads one slope (the doorstep of Building 06, the station's steps), the standing faces by quarter-metre columns so a facade's door stays open, the ground's level faces by half-metre cells so a yard's floor ends at its ramp, and ONE ROOF, ONE FLOOR for everything else — a shape's level faces that touch are one rectangle, the box around them, nothing cut or filtered inside it, so a body on a deck cannot fall through it (four falls in a day from the rules that cut: user, "how hard is it to make an un-fallable roof with simple cbox geometry"), nothing over 9 m, and the model's own audit (`box_audit.mjs`: the boxes that fill a place the model shows room in, 272 before, 119 after, most of them 8 m up or a porch) — user 2026-10-29, seven spots, "keep the cbox geometry simple, but they gotta match the visuals" — every spawn place and both homes on the one walked region, the homes 652 u apart by the grid (604 before), the small building's roof reached from its stair's landing by a jump as before (the roof the model's own 6.15 m top; the hand edits of 2026-10-28 are not re-applied, there being no slots to mend). A match of two Sudden Death bots 1.4 ms a tick (1.3 before). Read at the user's report that a bot at its home could not track the enemy at the other home: on the cell reading and on this one alike the route between the homes exists, and a bot at the east home walks it rather than sprints — the Sudden Death follower banks its boost while a jump lies ahead on the route, and the east home's routes carry two or three (the drops off its plot) — so it takes half a minute to cross; the plain brain sprints and arrives in twenty seconds. **The fourth pass (b1026t, the same day; user, after six rounds shipped without a look at the model: "remake the whole TOWN map again, with very simple geometry cboxes that MATCH THE VISUAL")** — THE SPLIT in the generator (tools README): a piece with walls and a roof is a box to its eave and its roof's slopes as planes over it, so a house's box stops where its roof begins, a body walks a dormer or a lean-to as the model shows it, and a flat box to the ridge (the annex of Building 02) is gone; a crown over a trunk is one box in the air (trees, lamps); a roof shape lies on its bottom and a ramp starts at its foot, so a car is one box and not planes over its hood; a wedge keeps the walls under its roof (the shed by Building 02, walked into from the side); a wall's single columns under a slope stay; a face set diagonally is cut into lengths (the billboard over the police yard was a 6 by 5 m block). And looked at before publishing, every region and every reported spot, with `box_view.cjs`: the model and the model with the data over it side by side — twenty regions, sixteen close-ups, the sheets given to the user — plus the audit's OVERSHOOT and MISSING WALLS checks (37 standing faces without a box, 4 after), walk probes at every reported spot (the shed's roof climbed from the front and its sides held, the stoops and the station's steps climbed in 6 cm steps, the garage passage walked through, the terrace and the north street without a drop), the tests (129 of 130; the one failing is Plain Field's sprint-latch test, failing before this work), the grid (15,917 nodes, both homes' routes, every spawn's), the cost (two Sudden Death bots 1.11 ms a tick mean, p99 13.5). 7,604 boxes and 8,389 surfaces (5,485 and 6,161 before: the walls by column under sloped roofs are many short boxes). **The skylight (b1026u, 2026-10-30; user: "it's a two way slopes … one thing to change and one thing only")** — the glass gable on Building 02's roof was one plane across its whole width (its RAMP spec); the spec's new form `^x` makes it two slopes from the eaves to the ridge, nothing else changed; looked at from four sides first, walked over the ridge both ways after. Then (b1026v, user: "the sides are not blocked so the unit walks into the cboxes") the gable's two ends as walls — thin boxes by quarter-metre columns up to the roof's height there, the triangle — so a body on the roof is held at the ends and still steps off the slope over them (the lips pass lids them); walked into both ends (held at the ridge, stepped over the 0.2 m corner by the eave), along the slope by the end and off it. **No fade on Town** (b1026x, 2026-10-30; user: "remove all transparent effect in Town") — `fade: false` on its GLB_MAPS line, a switch every modelled map can set: nothing on Town goes translucent, the three north objects' fade of b1026s–v included (with the fade keys merely gone, the cells' things were still registered — 245 of them — hence the switch). **The taxi (b1027a, 2026-10-30; user: "this taxi does not have a CB for its main body")** — the yellow taxi's body by the police station, 1,812 faces with skirt bevels down to its bottom, passed the pieces' roof-shape test and came out as two floors and no walls; `BOX=Object_77#3` in the map's recipe reads it as one box, as the other cars are. One part named, nothing else of Town's data changed. Then (b1027d, user 2026-10-30, "the first spot's CB does not reflect the underground slope visual"): Building 12's entrance ramp into the underground was the street floor; by map data alone (a RAMP plane, a HOLE in the street floor over it, two thin side walls — the generator's new WALL spec and HOLE's optional upper bound; no reading rule touched) it is now read like Building 11's, the user's sample: a slope from −0.63 to −2.55 with its sides, down to the doorway's block at its foot, verified from four angles and walked down, across (held by the sides) and back up. Since b1104i its two side kerbs are flush with the street and its side walls' tops are at −0.55 m (user 2026-11-04, "Lower the heights of these cboxes so the unit can walk over them into the underground slope."): a unit walks off the street over either side and drops onto the slope, and one on the slope is still held by the walls. The yellow tanker truck in the street (model x 2.67..12.71 z −27.61..−24.59) is solid since b1104a (user 2026-11-04: "Make the whole cboxes of this vehicle un-walkable for units. Currently bullets and unit can go through some of its cboxes. No need adjusting the shape of cboxes for this one.", then the bottoms brought down too): its 110 boxes are walls from the street — their plans as the reading made them, no top a body stands on, their bottoms down to the street — and the 106 floors and 4 slopes on it are gone, so no unit stands on it, steps into its slices or walks under its belly, and a round stops at it (`data_solid.mjs`, tools README). The car on the raised deck (model x 52.69..58.54 z 15.79..18.33, the deck 3.88 m over the street) is two boxes since b1104h, as Compound's car (user 2026-11-04, "Rework this vehicle with this kind of simple design."): its body to 5.20 m and its cabin to 5.90 m, both standing from the deck with lids a body stands on, in place of the reading's 6 boxes, 9 floors and 9 slopes (`data_boxes.mjs` with a base, tools README). Six street cars and vans the same way since b1104i (user 2026-11-04, "Apply this style to below other vehicles."), each a body box over the model's extent to its bonnet's height and a cabin box over its roof's span, both from the street: model x 28.17..30.80 z 26.96..31.53 (1.15 / 1.81 m), x 1.91..4.37 z 14.93..20.12 (1.30 / 1.85), the two police cars at x -13.39..-8.51 z -16.65..-14.17 (1.30 / 2.00) and x -13.35..-8.61 z -11.40..-8.92 (1.15 / 1.70), the police van at x -2.02..1.42 z -15.70..-9.87 (1.50 / 2.90) and x 18.76..21.18 z -15.41..-9.61 (1.20 / 1.90), in place of their 70 boxes, 66 floors and 16 slopes (wheels, bumpers and lamps among them).

**Spacecraft** (2026-10-19) — the second modelled map: *Among Us - Map "The Skeld"* by Pablopcb on Sketchfab, licensed CC-BY-4.0 ([the model](https://sketchfab.com/3d-models/among-us-map-the-skeld-59a93886f9e74ff6836dff0c269da45f)), served as `client/public/maps/spacecraft.glb` (11.8 MB: the vertex normals dropped, `glb_strip.mjs`, so three shades it flat; the whole scene scaled by 14 in the copy, `glb_rescale.mjs`). The model's own unit is tiny (the ship 8.4 × 5 units, its walls 0.16 tall), so the scale is set by its chairs: at **14 m per model unit** a chair is 1.75 m tall and 1.1 m wide, the walls 2.0–2.2 m, the corridors 3.5 m, the ship 118 × 69 m (472 × 277 units); the chairs alone would say 9, below 11 the walls fall under the body's hop height (1.5 m) and the ship stops being corridors, and 14 was chosen so the characters read smaller in it (user 2026-10-19). The same generator reads it with two parameters that are the map's own data, not rules: `UNDER_STREET` 3 (only space lies under its floors, where Town's terrain plates lie 12 cm under the street) and `CEIL` 1.3 — the map's ceiling: no face at or above it is read as a floor and nothing's top at or above it is stood on (the wall tops used to be walked as platforms, the crates climbed), a slim thing reaching it is a wall, not a fence (the ship's thin partitions are under the 2 m fence height), and the sim caps every body's feet at it (the ceiling rule), so nothing jumps onto or over a wall, Aris included; and a thing whose top is at or over the ceiling is a wall, not a standable thing — no hop passes over it (a body at the top of its hop used to sail over the 1.4–1.8 m parts of a machine and come down wedged among them). The two engine pits (2 m down, no ramp in the model) and a few floored slivers between walls stay unreachable.
