(1) A cell keeps sixteen levels (eight on the first day, four before), and over the cap the LOWEST stay: the cut fell on the lowest, so a stairwell of six flights stacked in one column kept its top four and no bot walked its ground floor (District); and the tallest building's column stands fifteen — floors every 4 m to its roof — so with eight kept no bot climbed past its eighth storey (user, the second day: "I'm standing on the rooftop of the highest building, and enemy BOT cannot find me; it's lingering at the ground floor"). (2) A walk edge's clearance is read along the walk, the body standing where the walk stands it at each unit of the way, instead of once at the lower floor's body height: a steep ramp was called blocked by the landing it climbs to, and no bot took District's stairs; the lip before a step (Factory's belts) is still met, the body standing on the ground there. (3) Drop links: a fall past the jump window, up to 50 m (15 until the second day: District's towers are 45 m, the sim has no fall damage, and the user wants the bot off their roofs), from a higher node to a lower one within two cells — one way, where a body walking off the higher node lands on the lower node's floor (a deck over the deck beneath it is no fall), the edge clear at the higher floor's body height or crossed by LIPS alone — a parapet, a sill, a rail that the jump's arc clears with nothing over the body's head (`onlyLipsAcross`, b1026j: a parapet was a wall to the links, and the bots took the stairs; user, the second day: "BOT doesn't seem to know it can jump out from windows or rooftop") — and within one walk component only where the walk round is 10 m or more; the follower vaults toward the waypoint a link leads to, so the bot jumps off and falls. A jump link between islands hops the same lips (a balcony's railing and the terrace behind it). District 3,933 drop links, Compound 1,594, Town 592, Dust 322, Warehouse 338, Hospital 102, Spacecraft 23. Routes read on District after: street to the balcony by its stair 141 m (none before), street to the stairwell's first floor 39 m (none before), the car park's top deck to the street beside it 58 m over three drops (72 m round the ramps before), the balcony to the street below 37 m with a drop (none before). The grid's dense arrays grew with the square of the layers a map needs — District's at fifteen were 35 MB in memory, and every search allocated 7 MB more — so since the second day the grid the searches read is THE NODES AS A LIST (`compactNavGrid`, b1026k): the walkable nodes alone, numbered by cell then by level, each cell's run of them, each node's cell, floor, point and clearance, and the walk and link neighbours as runs in two adjacency arrays; the builder still works in the dense form and compacts it at the end, the neighbours keep the dense walk's order, so every route is the same route (300 routes a map on five maps, searched again from their own ends: the same waypoints). The file is that list form verbatim (NAV_DATA_VERSION 4, `serializeNavGrid`; version 3 the same morning listed the dense form's nodes and filled the arrays back in at load): District's 3 MB in memory and on disk (12.6 MB on disk and 35 in memory at version 2), Compound's 0.5 (2.3 before); `navdata.test.js` reads every array back to the bit. See "The cost of District" below.

**The cap and the slice** (b1026h, user 2026-10-29, "I want the pathfinder to find the enemy when it needs — no matter how many levels there are, as long as the map connects; if the finding costs much, maybe expand the searching scale gradually as time goes") — two things in `navgrid.js` and the brains. THE CAP: a route or firing search gave up after a flat 30,000 pops; District's grid (78,779 nodes over fifteen layers) needs up to 44,000 for a route from the plaza to the bowling alley three storeys down, and 60,000 to learn that no route exists, so one route in four across the map ended as "no path" (80 random pairs measured) and a bot above never came down for an enemy in the cellars. A search now gives up after `SEARCH_POPS_PER_NODE` (2) pops per walkable node of its grid, 30,000 at the least — District 158,000 — so every route the map joins is found; `opts.maxPops` overrides. THE SLICE: a search that long runs whole in one tick nowhere now. `opts.popsPerCall` parks a search with a job after that many pops in one call, as the server's per-tick deadline did; the Sudden Death brain's approach route (`sdApproachRoute`, `me.botSDRouteJob`) runs 3,000 pops a tick (`SD.routePopsPerTick`, about 4 ms) and starts afresh when the enemy has moved 12 units from where it was aimed (`SD.routeRestartU`), keeping its plan while the search goes on — District's longest route takes 14 ticks, most a tick or two; the plain brain's route and firing searches slice the same way where the host sets no deadline (`BOT_SEARCH_POPS_PER_TICK`). Read on District after, with the escalator's phantom plane gone from the data (the District paragraph): the street's north-west corner to the bowling alley 292 m, the car park's top deck to the alley 306 m over one drop, B1 to B2 to B3 and back by the escalator and the twin ramps; of 80 random pairs of nodes, every pair the map joins is found. What stays apart is apart in the map: roofs with no stair (reached by drop links only, so a bot up there comes down and none goes up), and the station plaza's backdrop plates (the terrace behind the balcony's 0.84 m railing was apart too, until the lips of b1026j). The bots' equivalence tests are re-frozen at b1026h (`freeze_refs.py`): a search of more than one slice hands its route a tick or more later than the whole search did, and the frozen brains played the whole search.

**The cost of District** (b1026i, user 2026-10-29, "what's the cost running games in District?") — measured headless in node on the second day's build. To load: the model 4.5 MB (1,366 things, 2,026 draw calls, 147,000 triangles; 5.5 MB until its vertex positions became 16-bit integers over each thing's box — `glb_quantize.mjs`, KHR_mesh_quantization, the finest step 2 mm, b1026k; the client turns them back into floats as the model is cached, `dequantizeMapModel` in `main.js`, since the fade's piece builder reads a position buffer by index and the loader's interleaved shorts came up as a thicket of spikes in the first photo — the download is the smaller file, the rest of the client sees the float model), the collision data 1.9 MB in its own file, fetched with the model (`district_data.json`, b1026k — 9,326 boxes, 8,701 floors; Town's 1.0 MB; inside the bundle until then, which was 8.3 MB and is 1.4), the nav file 3 MB read back in a millisecond into 3 MB of arrays (the list form, b1026k; 0.15 s into 35 MB in the dense form the morning before), the data's signature — computed to check the file is this map's — 0.05 s. A route search allocates 0.7 MB of arrays (7 in the dense form) and runs whole in up to 93 ms (the street to a tower roof, 44,000 pops; the plaza to the bowling alley 41,000) or in slices of 4 ms a tick (the first slice 12 ms); a search that finds no route costs 60 to 70 ms whole; the firing search 68 ms. The first bot tick of a match took 6.8 s — the cover-distance pass over every node and every box, 735 M box distances — and takes 0.5 s now that it reads the boxes within 2 m of each node (`coverDistFor`; Town 0.1 s). A 1v1 of two Sudden Death bots: 1.6 ms of sim a tick on average (Town 1.3), the median 0.9 (0.45), one tick in a hundred over 21 ms (Town 17) — read on b1026k; the peaks are the brain's own route pricing and hide searches, the same on both maps, against the 16 ms tick, and the same reading varies by half between runs on the shared machine. Not measured: the client's frame — the fade tests 1,366 hulls a frame and the GPU draws 2,026 primitives, both about twice Town's.

**The maps' data as files** (b1026k, user 2026-10-29, "anyway we can cost down the run in District?") — the seven modelled maps' collision data (Hospital, District, Compound, Warehouse, Dust, Town, Spacecraft: `FILE_DATA_MAPS` in `arena.js`) left `arena.js` (96,790 lines to 10,532) for `client/public/maps/<mapKey>_data.json`, the boxes and floors alone; the spawn rows and the bounds stay in the table. The bundle went from 8.3 MB to 1.4 (gzipped 1.2 to 0.4), and a map's data is fetched with its model when the map is picked (`warmMapData` in `main.js`: District's 1.9 MB, Compound's 3.8, Town's 1.0, the browser caching it like the model) and registered (`registerGeneratedData`; `hasGeneratedData`, `generatedDataFile`); the match waits for it, so a modelled map's first match starts after one fetch more. The server's warm-up reads the file before building the arena; the tools and the tests load the files through `shared/tools/maps/arena_files.mjs`; `getArena` builds a map's arena the first time it is asked for, and throws a clear error for a modelled map whose data is not registered. `data_install.mjs <tree> <mapKey> <json>` installs a generator's reading as the file. Nothing in play changed: the same boxes and floors, the same nav files (their signatures are the data's), every sim test passing with the loader.

**A map's boost scale** (b1026m, user 2026-10-29, "for District only, 5 times every unit's boost gauge amount and 5 times the refill speed; also adjust the bots' cost gates accordingly") — `MAP_DATA.<map>.boostScale` in `constants.js` (`boostScaleOf`): on that map every unit's gauge volume and refill, and every boost threshold the bots and the command mode hold, are multiplied by it; the costs — a dash tick, a jump, a dodge, the sniper's cancel — stay. District: 5, so 1,250 of gauge and 23 a tick back, five dashes' worth of running on a 197 x 274 m block. The fighter carries the scale from its match's map (`state.js` `createFighter`, the respawns too), `tickBoost` in `movement.js` scales the cap and the regen, the plain brain its travel latch (250 and 200), hide-dash arm and drop (50 and 8), jump bank and sprint floors, the survival and commanded tiers (60), the Sudden Death brain its reserve (86), dash floor (8) and the full tank THE VALVE opens at, `command.js` and the client's command preview their floor, bank and arm, the client its own gauge and the HUD bar's maximum. Offline, online and the server alike: one rule, read off the map. Compound: 5 too (b1027a, user 2026-10-30, "same as District"), its card carrying the same note. Neon had 5 too, and the note, from 2026-11-01 until 2026-11-03 (user: "remove the 5 times boost feature in Blockade"): it plays at 1 again. Every other map — every concept map, every other scene map, and the Shooting Range (the client's alone, it has no row) — plays at `DEFAULT_BOOST_SCALE`, 1: its gauge and every gate are the unit's own numbers. (Every map took 5 for part of 2026-11-01 — user: "make all maps (scene and concept) grant units 5 times boost" — and went back the same day — user: "revert the boost amount and the BOT boost management gates to 1 time in other maps"; the three maps' notes came back with it. What the day left: the HUD bar and the command card's stamina bar read the gauge at the unit's scale — the card had read 250 as full, so on the three maps it sat full through the top four fifths of the gauge; online the client copies the server's scale off the snapshot, its mechs being built before the match's map is known.) The tests: `boost_scale.test.js` reads every map's scale, the cap and the refill, a Trio respawn's gauge, the travel leg, the Sudden Death valve and the commanded-travel latch at 5 against 1; `travel_sprint.test.js` the Pursue latch at both.

**Where a node stands** (b1024z8) — in the nav grid (`navgrid.js`): a node sits at its cell's centre when a body fits there, else a quarter cell (one unit) to one of the four sides, the first that fits (`standAt`; the point is kept per node in `posX`/`posZ` and read by every path builder through `nodeCenterX/Z`). The cells are a metre and the body 0.58 m across, so a lane narrower than two cells between two things holds a body while both rows of cell centres miss its clear band: Compound's 1.5 m catwalk between a kerb and a wall (the band 0.69 m wide, the two centres 4 and 27 cm outside it) and its 1.75 m mezzanine between a guard rail and a balustrade (0.88 m, missed by 9 and 3 cm). Both lanes were no floor to the bots, the covered bridge between the two buildings was an island of the grid, `findPathOnGrid` found no route to a player on it and the Sudden Death brain took the wait (user 2026-10-24: the bot that had tracked the player along the west walkway stood idle once the player crossed onto the bridge). The walk edges are read between the two nodes' own points — both arrivals and the fit at the midpoint — and the midpoint's fit stays the strict one: a variant that loosened it too (any of five points about the midpoint clear) was measured and dropped the same day, as it let 76 edges slip past Station's half-unit platform faces into the hollow platforms and halved the route between its homes. Measured over the twelve maps: every spawn-to-spawn route the same length or shorter (Factory 106 → 102 m, Warehouse 65 → 59 m), none longer; walkable nodes +1 to +14 % (Compound 18,502 → 19,703); the build 40–50 % slower (Compound 1.8 → 2.6 s, Town 0.6 → 0.85 s — once per match on the offline client, at boot on the server), a path search about a millisecond as before, 0.8 MB more grid on Compound. Half-metre cells would have opened the same lanes for 2.5 times the build time and four times the memory. Two more readings of b1024z10 — every walk edge swept at the lower floor's body height, and an island link with no least rise — are under Bot logic (Factory's belts).

**Spawn places** (b1021x) — a map's spawn places are its data: the homes `p1`/`p2` and, on a map that names them, `more` places (Town: six, user 2026-10-18; Dust, Backrooms and Warehouse: ten each from the user's marked plans, 2026-10-22). `pickRespawnSpawn` in `spawn.js` is the one rule for where a Trio respawn stands, asked by the server's Trio step and the offline client's `respawnSlotMech` alike (user 2026-10-22, "keep the respawned unit's contact with the enemy as late as possible"): a place no living enemy sees — sight by the bots' own test at a metre over the floor, the enemies where they stand at the moment of the death — drawn at random; none unseen, the farthest from the nearest enemy (a first tier of unseen places 12 m or more away was tried and dropped the same day: it left places never drawn). Teammates count for nothing (until 2026-10-22 a respawn joined a living teammate). A place within 8 units of a living body is passed over while another remains. A map may instead draw at random among its places (`pick: 'random'` in its spawns — Spacecraft alone, user 2026-10-19), a place with a living body on it still passed over. A map without `more` places keeps the slot's own start.

**What the second modelled map brought** (b1022a–g) — rules written for Spacecraft's geometry, in force on every map:

**Dead floor** (b1022e) — in the generator (`shared/tools/glb_collision.mjs`): a slot a body cannot stand in is not floor. A body centred in a cell covers the cells around it; a cell is stood in when none of those holds a thing more than a step over the floor, and a floor cell no stood-in cell covers is dead — a slot between two parts of a machine, the strip behind a railing set against a wall — and joins the tallest thing beside it (the railing against the wall is the wall), so no body comes down into it from the air to be pushed from one thing into the other. Town regenerated under it: 7192 → 7425 boxes, 7323 → 7415 floors (then 7278 and 6554 under the narrow-floor and parapet rules); Spacecraft's walk components 120 → 28. Known cost: such a slot blocks rounds and sight too. The same for a floor the narrow-floor rule took back with nothing standing on it (b1022r): the ledge outside the south-east terrace's parapet, a cell wide between the parapet and the rim's drop, stayed a thing at its own height, its box standing from the terrace floor beside it (higher than its top), and a body walked onto it from the terrace's corner, fell into the slot and hung between the rim and the parapet (user 2026-10-19); joined to the parapet, the slot is parapet. And a thing no higher than the lowest walked floor beside it stands from the ground, never from that floor: no box whose bottom is over its top.

**Narrow floor, parapet** (b1022n) — in the generator too: a floor a body cannot stand on is not floor. Town's east terrace has a parapet along its south and west edges (a metre thick, 0.9 m up, a 3.5 m drop beyond); the flood stepped onto its corner block by a 0.6 m lip and along the parapet's top, the kerb rule laid ramps onto it, and a body walked up and off the terrace. Now a reached floor cell is stood in only when every cell a body's radius around it has a reached floor within a step of its height, and one no stood-in cell covers gives its reach back (a railing's top, a ledge a hand wide: what stands there is a thing again). And a **parapet** — a reached floor cell no cell GROUND_MIN/2 (0.75 m) wide around it at its height covers, standing a hop over a walked floor within 1.5 m on one side and over a drop of more than 1.5 m on another — gives its reach back and is low cover without a top: a body walking stops at it, a hop crosses it (a low wall is jumped, user 2026-10-19), nothing stands on it, so no one walks off the terrace by it and no bot perches on it. The same for a parapet the flood never reached — a thick low thing a hop up (the south-east terrace's rim), which used to carry a top a body hopped onto and walked off: a thing narrower than GROUND_MIN, with a walked floor a hop under it within GROUND_MIN on one side and a floor more than KERB_MAX under it on another, carries no top. A planter a step up on every side and a catwalk over drops on both sides are what they were. Both maps regenerated.

**A roof's slab over a passage** (b1022q) — in the generator: a ceiling over a walked passage whose top lies within SLAB of a standable top or a deck beside it is that roof's slab over the passage — the model's height (a ceiling box used to top out a metre class above it: an invisible lip to a body on the roof beside it), a floor on it (there was none: a hole), hopped like the roof, the passage walked under it. Town's south-edge garages: open ground floors under roofs a body hops onto from the terrace beside them (user 2026-10-19).

**A slope's body** (b1022s) — in the generator: a slope (a road's ramp, a flight of steps, a kerb's ramp, a pitched roof) has a body beneath it as a deck has, from the ground — or the roof of a walked passage under it — up to the plane's lowest corner, when that is a step or more. A slope used to be a surface in the air with its skirts the only solid under it; the model's houses are hollow shells, and a body that walked under a hip roof's eave from the ledge beside it (the south-edge garages' house) fell into the house and hung there (user 2026-10-19). A skirt now reads the plane on the rectangle's edge, not a cell beyond it, where a steep eave read a step shorter than it was and went unskirted. Town 7330 → 8804 boxes, Spacecraft 3341 → 3373.

**A parapet's two sides, a slim thing's seam** (b1022x) — in the generator: a parapet stands between the floor and the drop, so the hop is on one side and the drop on the side opposite (both the cell rule and the thing rule; the thing rule reads the drop from what stands beside the thing — a neighbouring thing's top, else the walked floor — a roof's top being no layer of the raster). A strip of the west terrace's pavilion roof, with the stair's landing a hop under it on one side and the terrace far below beyond its end, read as a parapet and lost its top; a body crossing the roof fell through it to the ground under the pavilion (user 2026-10-19). And a slim thing whose top lies within a step of a floor beside it — a roof's ridge, the step between two roof slopes, a quarter of a metre wide — is that floor's seam and carries the floor across at its own top; it used to carry none, as a railing's top carries none (a railing stands beside no floor at its height: still no top). Town 6595 → 7499 floors.

**The map's ceiling** (b1022b) — a modelled map may carry a ceiling (`ceil` in `MAP_BOUNDARY`, from the generator's `CEIL`): `integrateFighter` caps a body's feet at it — a jump stops there and Aris's flight holds there — in the shared sim and the offline client's mirror alike, so nothing lands on or crosses a wall taller than the ceiling. Spacecraft's is 1.3 m (the walls are 1.6–1.76 m); Town and the hand-made maps have none and are untouched. Known cost: a jump on Spacecraft peaks at 1.3 m instead of 1.4 m.

**A body never rests in a box** (b1022e) — `resolveUnitObstacleCollisions` (physics.js, and the offline client's mirror) puts a body back where the tick began when its pushes leave it still overlapping a box by more than a twentieth of a unit: in a slot narrower than the body (two parts of a machine a hand apart, the strip behind a railing set against a wall) the pushes used to conflict, land the body inside a thin box, and the push to that box's nearest face flung it on through the next — a body wedged in a wall. Now it hangs at the slot's lip until its input takes it elsewhere. Where the tick began must have been clear itself (a body already in a box keeps the old pushes). `shared/test/rest.test.js` drops a body into a slot.

**The floor after the walls** (b1022x) — in the shared tick (`shared/src/sim/tick.js`): the horizontal move, the walls' push-out, then the vertical step — gravity, landing, the floor under the body (`integrateHorizontal` / `integrateVertical`, `movement.js`). Read before the walls, the floor was looked up where the body tried to go: a body walking into the wall at a roof's edge stood with its centre a hair inside the wall's footprint, where no floor lies, was marked airborne and fell a little, the wall pushed it back, and the next tick the same again — it sank through the roof while standing still (user 2026-10-19). The offline client has always stepped the physics, resolved the walls, then read the floor; the tick now agrees.

**Spawns:** Station spawns sit in the platforms' far corners (±128, ±112 on the decks — the old track-corridor spawns anchored every fight to the railway axis), and Streets spawns sit at the diagonal corners (±126, ±82). Airport spawns sit on the ground at the mouth of a corner ramp (±130, ±56 — ramp feet at |z| 50), close enough that every bot climbs to the security plateau within seconds and the fight happens up top. 2v2 teammates offset along **X** on Station, Airport and Flashpoint so both members start side by side and equidistant from the objective; Streets and Factory offset along Z toward the map centre (a plain +Z would bury the teammate in the boundary wall). Keeping the pair equidistant is what matters: an offset that leaves one team nearer the objective hands that team the opening and pins the loser on the ground for the match. Lobby spawns sit against the side walls (±83, 38; teammates +12 along Z), each pair screened by its own **spawn counter** — a 9.4-tall check-in island 13u toward the centre that blocks every cross-map spawn sightline, so engagements start with an approach around either end instead of an instant stare-down.

**Scrapyard** (the scrapyard retheme of Factory 2 — internal key stays `factory2`) keeps the industrial remake's bones on Airport's design philosophy: one central organizing anchor — a raised **scrap-plank terrace** with four walk-up ramps plus jump-through fence openings (two 16-wide mid-side gaps and four corner notches) that bots use too, via the pathfinder's shortcut links — surrounded by dense, trustworthy cover (shanty huts, lived-in containers, upright cargo hulks, sheet-metal fences, market stalls) that passes the sizing rules everywhere: true cover is 8+ tall with real depth, vault clutter stays under 2.5. Two low walkable shack-row roofs (a jump up — no ramp) flank the terrace; the terrace itself is ramp-accessible from all four sides and nothing on the map is flight-only. The layout is point-symmetric, the retheme is visual-only (collision boxes are byte-identical to Factory 2, checksum-verified against the shared export), and its online collision data is exported from the offline builder with the `__exportArenaCollision('factory2')` console hook (re-run after any geometry change) so both modes stay identical.

## Project layout

- `client/` — Three.js + cannon-es + Vite frontend. Both offline match runtime and online client live here (`src/online/` holds the socket connection and the debug panel).
- `server/` — Node + Socket.IO authoritative game server. Multi-lobby, runs the shared simulation per match.
- `shared/` — pure-JS game logic that the server (and the online prediction layer in the client) consumes. State, physics, AI, projectile system.
- `render.yaml` — Render blueprint (free-tier deploy of both services).
- `PLAN.md` — implementation history / phased roadmap.
- `DIORAMA_PLAN.md` — design record for the diorama / command mode (offline phases and the online migration).
- `docs/img/` — the lock-range and weapon renders embedded above.
- `shared/test/` — the `node --test` suites CI runs.
- `.github/workflows/ci.yml` — `npm ci` + `npm test` on every push / PR.

## Local development

Install workspace dependencies from the repo root:

```
npm install
```

Run the server (terminal 1):

```
npm run dev:server
```

Run the client (terminal 2):

```
npm run dev:client
```

Run the shared-sim tests (what CI runs on every push / PR):

```
npm test
```

Open <http://localhost:5173>. The offline menu lets you pick a unit and play immediately. **Online (vs Player)** connects to the server you started in terminal 1.

## Deployment (Render)

The repo ships with `render.yaml` defining two free-tier services:

- `gvg-server` — Node web service (the Socket.IO server)
- `gvg-client` — static site (the Vite-built client)

After the first deploy, set the client's `VITE_SERVER_URL` environment variable in the Render dashboard to your server's public URL (e.g. `https://gvg-server-xxxx.onrender.com`) and redeploy the client. Both services are `autoDeploy: true`, the server exposes `/health` for Render's health check, and `render.yaml` bakes a placeholder `VITE_SERVER_URL` into the static build — the dashboard value overrides it.

> Free-tier services spin down after ~15 min idle. First connection to a cold server takes 30–60 s. Acceptable for prototype testing, not for production. The warm-up reads each modelled map's grid file (see *The route grid as data* below) instead of building it — until b1025c it built every grid at boot, Compound's ~3.4 s on a fast core and a minute on the free instance, and neither the health check nor a handshake was answered meanwhile; the log says `grid <map>: file, N ms` per map.
>
> **The client waits it out (2026-10-25, the user's "web socket error").** Until then the client tried five times 600 ms apart and gave up, leaving "Connection error: websocket error" on the screen for anyone who arrived while the server was waking. `client/src/online/connection.js` now retries without limit, 1–5 s apart with jitter (`reconnectionAttempts: Infinity`, `reconnectionDelay` 1000 → `reconnectionDelayMax` 5000), and counts the attempts; the online overlay reads "Waking the server up… Ns — attempt N" for the first two minutes and names the last error after that, still retrying. Nothing changed on the server: the wait is as long as it was, only no longer fatal. Shortening it is the route-grid precompute on the Compound list.

## Implementation notes

- **Server authoritative.** Shared sim runs on the server at ~62.5 Hz; clients predict their own local fighter and reconcile against snapshots.
- **The input queue (user 2026-10-25, "the backtrace while sprinting").** The server used to keep one frame per player — each arriving frame overwrote the last and was acknowledged on arrival — and apply whatever it held once a tick; two frames arriving within one tick meant a step the client had predicted and the server never took, and the next snapshot pulled the player back by it (0.44 u at a sprint). On a server whose ticks come late and bunched (the free instance's CPU quota is pulsed) that was most frames, most of the time. The frames now queue per player (`shared/src/sim/inputqueue.js`) and the tick takes one, in order, acknowledging only what it took: a late server means a step applied late, not a step lost. Two bounds: past 4 waiting frames a tick takes two, merged (the later frame's movement, every tap of both — a skipped step, but only while the server is that far behind); past 16 the oldest are dropped as taken. An empty tick leaves the last frame standing with its taps cleared, as before; a start hold drains the queues unapplied. The client is unchanged — the acknowledgement means what it always meant, only now it is true.
- **Lag compensation (2026-10-17).** A human's shot is judged in the world their screen showed. Every input frame carries `viewAt`, the `serverTime` of the snapshot the client had drawn when the frame was made; the server turns it into the shooter's lag (0–100 ms, `LAG_COMP_MAX_MS`; 200 for its first day, 100 on the owner's call) and keeps the last 144 ms of every body — position and the two immunity clocks — in `shared/src/sim/history.js`, recorded each tick after the bodies move. A round, pellet, beam or sweep fired with a lag aims at and is tested against the target's body of that many milliseconds ago, over its whole flight; the damage lands on the live body. Bots, offline and older clients carry no `viewAt` and read the live body, unchanged (the Health Point and Sudden Death equivalence tests still pass tick for tick). The cost is the usual one — favor the shooter: a hit can land up to 100 ms after the target reached cover on its own screen, and bots, who see the present, give a laggy human that much. Before this the server judged every shot in its present while the shooter's own round was drawn against the enemy as last seen, one network leg behind, so a round seen to land could do nothing and a round seen to miss could hit. The line as it stood before this change is commit f5c2755 (2026-10-17).
- **Bot AI** has one logical state machine (Defense > Maze > Engage > Pursue) in one module, `shared/src/sim/nbrain.js`. The online server (`tickBot` in `shared/src/sim/ai.js`) and the offline client (`updateEnemy` in `client/src/main.js`) are its two hosts: each supplies the enemy views and the command entry, and pays out the momentum and runs the gauge itself. The body rules after the brain — the reload hold and the hit-stun on the velocity the brain set — are one shared function (`botBodyRules` in `ai.js`, b1104d, user 2026-11-04, "Implement option B"), which both hosts run before the momentum is paid out, and both hold the payout back while a dodge's step runs. Until then the offline client kept its own copy in the other order (a sprinting bot it stunned lost the sprint's carried speed as well, which the server's kept) and paid the momentum out mid-step (a Sudden Death dodge started inside the brain carried the sprint through its lerp, which the server's held). Since b1104e the brain banks a sprint's momentum at the stun's move-scale (`hitStunMoveScale` in `movement.js`), so a stunned sprinting bot is slowed in full on both hosts, as a player.
- **Universal pathfinder.** Maze is route-first: a nav grid is derived from each map's collision data (4-unit cells, A* plus a firing-position search), with jump-links bridging separated walk islands (e.g. Station's raised platforms) so bots climb instead of grinding walls. The old heuristic wall-following was retired once the grid covered every map; no-route ticks now run a minimal steer with a stuck-memory bias, re-planned on every stall signal. The grid is **multi-layer**: a cell can carry several stacked floors (the Streets bridge deck and the road beneath it are different nodes), so bots route under an overpass instead of treating the whole column as one surface. Cells are graded by how much room a body actually has, and A* pays extra to enter a tight one — a price, never a ban, so a genuinely narrow chokepoint costs more and still gets used. Planned routes are then **smoothed into diagonals** where it's provably safe: a run of grid legs collapses into one straight leg only when a swept corridor test passes. Doorways, ramps, jump-links and clutter alleys fail that test by design and keep their grid legs; open-field staircases become the diagonal you'd expect. Bots hold a per-weapon range band centered on their lock range (sweet spot ±7) — one rule for every weapon; in 2v2 the band derives from the compressed `lockRange2v2` value instead.
- **Bullets vs decks and ramps** (humans and bots alike). A walkable surface stops any round that **crosses** it — a shot fired under a bridge deck stays under it, and one fired into a slope dies on the slope. The test is exact: the segment is clipped to the surface's footprint and its height compared against the surface's at the two clipped ends, which is complete because both vary linearly along the ray. It replaced an 8-sample sign-flip walk that was blind to any crossing falling between samples — and a round covers ~34 units in a single tick at 2000 u/s, so fast shots used to pass straight through the Streets bridge slope and hit whoever stood on it. Surfaces are still judged one at a time over their own footprint, so a level shot passing OVER a sidewalk and UNDER the bridge deck cannot "flip" across two unrelated slabs.
- **Stamina economy** is shared by humans and bots — same cap (250), drain (1.1/tick), regen (4.59/tick), and empty-recovery lockout. On a map with a `boostScale` — District and Compound: 5 — the cap, the regen and every bot threshold below are that many times the numbers given here — 1,250 of gauge, 23 a tick back — while the costs stay; every other map plays at 1. See **A map's boost scale** under Maps. Bot decisions layer a **travel sprint latch** on top (2026-09-29): a Pursue / Maze sprint leg **arms only from a full tank (250)** and **runs down to 200**, then the bot walks until the tank is full again; discretionary jumps (the pursue elevation aids, Maze route jumps) fund from the same 200 line, so at least 200 stays banked for the survival reflexes. Until then the arm and the release both sat on the 250 reserve, which in practice meant a one-tick dash every half second in Maze (the flickering sprint pose) and no travel sprint at all in Pursue. Two survival exemptions spend through the reserve — Defense escapes under live fire (down to the hard floor of 8, then a walk until the gauge is back over **50** before it sprints again — the **escape sprint latch**, b1104b, user 2026-11-04, one rule with the Hide stance's dash and the cover move's) and the anti-glint dodge (gated only by the unit's raw step cost — 48 by default; step cost/duration/cooldown/distance are per-unit tunable like the jump family). **Mandated jumps** — the Defense hop/vault and command-mode route jumps — fire from a flat **60 boost** (2026-08-22) rather than the reserve; every other jump keeps the 200 gate. The latch is purely a decision threshold; the mechanics underneath stay human-identical.
- **Friendly fire** in 2v2 is off — bullets pass through teammates.
- **Per-team snapshot filtering (2026-08-21).** The server builds each snapshot in two team variants (plus a spectator view) and emits per socket: enemy fighters ship with the boost value redacted and every bot-intent field stripped, and each team's variant carries only its **own** commanders' standing orders for teammate rendering. Command state lives in a side-table off the fighter objects, so it cannot leak into a snapshot by construction.
- **Command layer (shared).** The order validation, latched dash travel, anchor orbit and force-lock rules live once in `shared/src/sim/command.js` with their tunables in `shared/src/sim/constants.js` (`CMD_*`) — the offline client and the server authority read the same source, and the client uses the same pathfind checks for its instant order preview/deny. Orders travel on dedicated messages (`order:move` / `order:lock` / `order:stance` / `order:clear`) that the server re-validates and acks, rate-limited to one per 500 ms per ordered unit (a commander driving a bot teammate can order both units back-to-back); `order:clear` is exempt. Travel is pathfinder-guided end to end (2026-08-22): the route re-plans from the current position at every reflex exit and on a 1.5 s cadence (each refresh re-passing the full order validation), and the driver executes the path's jump-links itself — vaulting at the mandated 60-boost tier (300 at the boost scale of 5, District's and Compound's), banking dash spend to 70 (350) when a jump lies ahead, and steering its own jump arcs toward the waypoint until landing.
- **Map collision data** for the online server is exported from the offline arena builder by hand — `__exportArenaCollision('<mapKey>')` in the browser console, pasted over that map's entry in `GENERATED_ARENA_COLLISION_DATA` (`shared/src/sim/arena.js`) — and must be re-run after any geometry change. Visual mesh is always rendered by the offline arena-build code on the client.
- **Pre-game loading.** Every unit visible in a pick (offline pickers) or in the lobby config (online queue room) starts its sprite-art downloads immediately — menu dead time absorbs the network wait — and GPU uploads are drip-fed one texture per frame. At match load, each Trio slot's 2nd/3rd roster units are additionally pre-built as complete (hidden) mechs, so a mid-match respawn is a pure swap-in: no construction, no decode, no upload during the fight.
- **Projectile broadphase (online sim).** Each map's obstacle boxes are indexed once into a 24-unit ground grid; every tick, each projectile (bullets, sniper rounds, laser bolts, every shotgun pellet) tests only the obstacles near its own flight segment for that tick instead of the whole map. The precise sweep test stays the final authority, so hit results are bit-identical to a full scan (differential-verified on all maps) — but dense maps (Factory: 400 boxes, 404 with the boundary walls) now cost the same as open ones, which removed the server-side lag during shotgun / high-RPM fights. Kei's hitscan beams and all non-weapon scans (bot sight, pathfinding, movement) deliberately keep the plain full scan.

- **The route grid as data (b1025c, user 2026-10-25).** A modelled map's nav grid is built once by `shared/tools/maps/navgrid_build.mjs` and served beside its model as `maps/<mapKey>_nav` (`NAV_FILE_EXT`: `.nav` on the release lines, `.wasm` on the PT site); the server's warm-up (`server/src/warmup.js`) and the client (`ensureOfflineNavGrid`, fetched at the map pick) read it instead of building — Compound's build was 3.4 s on a fast core and a minute on the free instance, a freeze no handshake survived (the "websocket error" at boot) and the offline match-start stall. The file carries a signature of the collision data it was built from (every box's and floor's fields), and a file whose signature is not the arena's is refused and the grid built; `shared/test/navdata.test.js` rebuilds every shipped file and compares it field for field, so a regenerated map or a changed builder fails the tests until the files are regenerated. Sizes: Compound 2.33 MB, Dust 0.75, Town 0.51, Spacecraft 0.32, Warehouse 0.09; a client downloads the one map it plays. For the file to be the client's grid too, the offline client's modelled arena now plays the shared obstacle list box for box — the server's perimeter slabs (`makeBoundaryObstacles`: 2 thick, 32 high, `topBuffer` 32) and the generated boxes with their floor tops marked — where it used to raise its own 28-high walls over the generated boxes (the two sims differed along the edges, a divergence noted in arena.js on 2026-10-09); the red edge line is drawn as before. `markFloorTops`, the arena's first-use pass that marks the boxes a floor lies on, asks the point index for the floors near each box instead of walking all of them: 811 → 133 ms on Compound, the same boxes marked (the count and the set checked on four maps).
- **Snapshots without unit tables (user 2026-10-25, the 5 GB month).** Every fighter in every snapshot carried its whole unit table (582 bytes, a quarter of a 2v2 snapshot); `buildSnapshotFor` now sends `unitKey` only and the client re-attaches the shared table on arrival (`connection.js`), as its prediction clone always did. 9,970 → 7,300 bytes per own-team 2v2 snapshot, 609 → 446 KB/s per client. The bot-intent fields and the rate are unchanged.
- **The lock moves to the living enemy (user 2026-10-25).** When the enemy a player is locked on dies and its Trio slot respawns, the lock moves to the other living enemy instead of following the slot to the fresh spawn. `applyInput`'s auto-retarget (2026-08-12) meant to do this on the death, but the server refills the slot in the very tick the unit dies, so by the player's next frame the slot held a living unit again and the lock went, silently, to the respawn behind its immunity; `respawnFighterNext` now moves every opponent's lock off the refilled slot when another living enemy exists (`shared/test/respawn_lock.test.js`). With none — Trio-1v1, a dead teammate — the lock keeps the slot as before. The offline client's `respawnSlotMech` already did this.

## Status

Prototype. Phases 0–3 from `PLAN.md` are landed in code (boot, sim extraction, networking, prediction & interpolation — PLAN.md's own status lines for phases 2–4 were never updated); Phase 4's robustness checklist is still open. 2v2 mode and the Duel / Trio main-mode split (Trio = three-unit stock rosters with in-place respawns) have been added on top of the original 1v1 scope, both offline and online. The Command view mode (see its section above) is playable offline and online per `DIORAMA_PLAN.md`.
